BOWLING,

WARRINGTON WORKS ASSOCIATION. POSTPONEMENT OF THE FINAL.

Another popular event had to he postpored on Eaturday on account of the heavy rainfall all day, It was intended to play the final game in the Finlay- son Cup Competition between Fletcher's Moulders and Howley Tamery, but the green was totally unfit for play. After a long consultation of the committee, Mr. Willett anncwaced that in view of the state a the green, and the fact that the committee had pone to great expense in providing the best gold medal for the money at their disposal, they had no other alterna- tive but to postpone the final until Saturday, the 7th I of September, when the arrangements made for last Saturday would remain intact. At the invitation rf Captain George Crosfield, a large number of officials and the members of both t-atus along with wives and -friends sat down to a substantial repast provided by Mr. and Mrs. llushworth. Tea over, a most enjoy- -able social evening was spent, and songs, &c. were admirably given, All the games in the handicap down for last Saturday were postponed until mxt Saturday. MI handicap games down for to-day will be played as originally arranged and were given on August 10th in the " Examiner," but the players would greatly assist the committee if they put in an appearance half-an- hour earlier than the time originally stated. The second round in the competition will be commenced on September 14th. The games for next Saturday are appended:

RIFLE SHOOTING.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB V. HALTON RIFLE CLUB.

The latter club visited the -Appleton club's range on Saturday last, to complete their return fixture. Tha match took place in dull and showery weather, and the light was not favourable to good shooting. The conditions were :—Eight men aside, the best six scores to count ; tea shots at a decimal target, at 25, 50, and 100 yards ; possible score at each distance, 100 The result was a victory for the Appleton club, by 27 points. Tea was provided on the range. The Halton team expressed themselves as being thoroughly pleased with their first visit to the Appleton range. BACI TO GAOL.

An old offender named John William Hayes, of no fixed abode, appeared before the Widnes magistrates on Monday on a °barge of stealing 2781 h of coal from a waggon on a siding it West Bank dock, the property of the Hutchinson Trustees. Hayes pleaded for another chance, as he only came out of prison on Saturday, and was starving. He said he hid nowhere to go, and was seeking somewhere to rest his head for the night. The bench committed him to goal for three months with bard labour, remarking that be had been given plenty of chances. He had a bad record.

FOOTBALL NOTES.

By " Ovest."

Warrington and Huddersfield will have the opening cf the football season to themselves on Monday. and the match will doubtless an use considerable interest as affording an indication of the form of the two team-. Wei ringion's prospects have already been freely dis- cussed, and while the out!ook for the season generally may be considelecl much more hopeful, the chances at Huddersfield are hatdly worth considering.

Huddersfield are relying on practically the same side whlch performed with such brilliance last season, abed cart ied off the League championship. They have not pone in for " star " platers on the lavish scale' of Wigan end Oldham, probably considering that they have enough talent already. At any rate the team of last season should be good en ugh to make an impres- sion on this season's coinpe;idoos. I uaderstand that Wrigley is nt plaor.g on Monday, and that Bartneloinew is on the transfer list.

The tetttn 'Warrington have selected to o:,-,pose fluddetstield at Fart,wn ia as follows : Jolley, full- hack; Bradshaw, Renick, rtickenson and Clare, threequarters ; Dairrith and Brooks, half backs ; Chester, Tranter, Fearnley, Sketh rn, Arnold and Critcbley, forwards. Reserve, Worrell.

T ern sotry I cannot entl use about Warrington's initial thirteen, but circumstances have conspired to prevent the stroi gest selection being made. Nlclntyre is aw y on holiday, Stiugars is still in South Wales, and Thomas has not yet signed on. The forwards are prnbably The lightest hit Warrington have ever put in the field, and it will take ail their youth and enthusiasm to c• pa with the big men of Huddersfield. I cannot see them tiettiog the ball v.iy often, which meat a that Warringiuu will probably spend a con- siderable time on the defence. ===== 0002601_19120831_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
KING MANUEL PRESENTS PRIZES TO FIREMEN.

The twenty-fifth anniversary of the National Fire* Brigades Union was celebrated at the. 2rystal Palace bn Saturday. Over 1,000 fire- men from all parts of the country were ?resent. Under .their president, the Earl of

Peckforton Hills are even further. fo the right is a sight not easily forgotten. The tops of the Mors just above Buxton are in full view, and at the very summit is a lonely farmhouse-looking place. It is the cele- brated "Cat and Fiddle Inn, 1,690 feet above the sea, actually the highest inn in all England, and strange to say, in the " flat" county of Cheshire. It may interest the Stockton Heath folks to know that it is, like themselves, included in the Knutsford I Parliamentary Division. At Bosley, refreshments may be obtained either at licensed iota or at a C.T.C. rest house where accommodabion for 40 is provided. The return may be wade through Congleton and the round is 56 miles I must ask the editor to correct a mistake made last week in setting up my article. I wrote that "we were badly diddled at the -- Hotel," For Bode contains several hotels - tso at least of the highest class, with "inspection pits," you know. It was at a much more modest one, where we stayed. But Bude is bonny, for all that. It is one of those unfortunate places that have done their best to get on, and develop into • a sea port, but the forces of nature and circumstances have brought all such efforts to nought. Thousands of pounds must have been spent early in the last century in making the harbour and the canal to Launceston with the necessary reservoir on the Tamar; now the canal is utterly useless; it is everywhere filled up with grass or rushes, and only the hunch- backed bridges and the track by the side, now only footpaths, testify to the sunk fortunes of a good many folk Who deserved better things. The harbour is, ' however, constantly used, though difficult to enter, and the breakwater forms a splendid promenade for the modern sea-faring men who go down to the sea to the ships, but take good care not to venture further, who do business beside the great waters, but not in them. So that anyone who wants a change from llfracowbe might try Bode. I clin't think they would be disappointed. Launceston, where we stayed the next night, is the county town of Cornwall, and well worthy of the dis- tinction. Its castle and grounds preserved by the Corporation make a splendid park,its church is unique in its carving, and we were agreeably astonished to find a town so well worth visiting. But don't be afraid of hill climbing. The next day was spent on Dartmoor. The wind blew at our backs, and carried Us up miles of hills, for the roads at least sere worthy of the beautiful county. Strange to say, the only fly in the ointment was the Devonshire hedges. Fifteen feet is a good height for a lady to stretch, even on a safety bicycle, and since the decline of the good Old Ordinary, even a man cannot hope to see over hedges like that. And when they keep on for scores of miles they get monotonous. Even on the edge of Dartmoor where our view ought to have carried the eyes to the top of Exmoor twenty miles away, it was only at the gates where we could enjoy it. Exeter is another city entirely worthy of an English- man's pride. It was here that the C.T.C. list failed us. Remembering the good fortune attending us at Llandovery and Appledore, we would try one of the boarding-houses mentioned in .that list After half-an-hour's hunt we found the house. We fled, and ran back to the Bridge Cafe, there to find Devon- shire fare at its best. We spent a most enjoyable evening in the Northernbay Park, and came to the conclusion that those who live at Exeter are exceed- ingly fortunate persons. Next morning, after spend- » couple of hours photographing we started northwards up the Exe valley. A violent shower was our first experience which spoiled the roads for us. We had got to the usual quagmire zone again, and the whole of that Friday, those shoddy surfaces were with us. But we were in Devonshire, and of all places in that beautiful corner of England, can any heat the valley of the Exe ? I don't know, but I doubt it.

SUFFRAGETTE SCENE AT HORSE SHOW. When the Lord Lieutenant was about to ft-mut the steps of the Vice-Regal box at the Dublin Horse Show on Wednesday, he was accosted by a suffragette about 20 years of age, who said : " Lord Aberdeen, are you aware that there are women in Mount joy Prison being forcibly fed " " Oh, it is all right," replied his Excellency. Several constables seized the young lady and conducted her from the grount's.

Londesborough, the men were inspected by King Manuel, who also presented the prizes. The Union has a membership of over 15,000. Our photograph shows King Manuel return- ing from the parade.

• The Club has not been in a hurry to sign on new men, and the only one of note so far secured is Dixon, the Cumberland lad, and formerly of Seaton, a club which produced Tom Fletcher, and more than one player of renown. Dixon is a likely looking lad, as hard as nails, and with somewhat of a reputation in the annual " guides " race at Grasmere. A man who can cover this punishing mountain course at racing speed will not cry " bellows "in any class of football. Dixon's trials have promised well for either half or threequarter play, and only an injury sustained last week prevented him turning out on Wednesday evening.

I hear that negotiations are at present si!ispended in regard to the Normanton half tack who turned out last Saturday. I have a high opinion of this lad, and so have those Warrington players who opposed him in the cup-tie last season. In flying after higher game, hope toe chance of securing the hardy young collier will not be lost. Warrington are not the only club who have got an eye on him ; and there is no question that the club is hard-up for half backs. Imagine Brooks injured again, and where are we?

In addition to Dixon, the only new players so far signed are, ii Valentine, a big young forwaul from Wigan district ; J Gallop and Bram wood of flylands. The committee are hoping to secure this week-end the signature of a well-known Welsh half-back who has been associated with Batley and Halifax. They are also in touch with a centre and wing from the Cumberland district, and hope to have both available for next week.

Yesterday, Daintith announced himself unable to play on Monday, and the vacancy at half-back will probably be given to Gallop, who has shaped well in the trials. This early change points to the necessity fir both the .Norrnanton and halifax halves being secured while the chance is yet open.

There will be a practice —r-Qch for the " A" team and juniors this afternoon.

PUT OUT OF THE WAY.

At St. Helens on Monday, a local labourer, John Williams, alias Walker, was committed for six months' bard labour for stealing boots and boot lasts, or the value of £5, from the shop of Jacksons, Ltd., 24,Church- street, on the 17th inst. The Shop manager left the premises secure at 11.15 at night, and the next morn- ing the police touhd that a large plate-glass window had been broken and the goons taken. The previous night a constable arrested Williams for being drunk and disorderly, and subsequent inquiries led to the discovery of the stolen goods at his lodgings. When charged, the prisoner said he had been standing in the shop doorway with a quart .bottle of beer in his arm, and be broke the window accidentally. He cut his hand, took the boots and lasts in two lots, which he bid in his bedroom. Prisoner said he was sorry for what he had dose. He had been steady for some weeks, but met a friend and had some beer. After breaking the window he took the boots home to put them out of the way.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
DEAD AND DYING RACES.

ECOPYIIIGHT.I

The word raco is an elastic term. It can be Applied to the great divisions of mankind, over the classification of which even the greatest ethnologists differ, but which can be roughly classed according to colour as white, yellow, and black, or more definitely as .Caucasian, Mon- golian, Ethiopian, American and Malay. Or, on the other hand, the term :may be applied more particularly to such smaller divisions, nations, or tribes as have varying characteris- tics to differentiate them one from another.

Races as a whole are, of course, subject to the same laws as individuals. Given favourable circumstances and environment, a race may go on thriving and flourishing for an apparently unlimited time. as, for instance, the Chinese; albeit the race has been rejuvenated, so to speak, by repeated incursions of the Manchus and other Mongol tribes. But races, just like their constituent units, families, are subject to adverse eircumstanc. They may be blotted out by famine or disease or war. 'They may lose their individuality by conquest or through being overwhelmed by weight of numbers. THE PASSING OF THE ABORIGINE.

Ultimately, however, the orocess of extinction appears to be an economic process. _ Nomadic and hunting -peoples require large areas of land over which to roam, and as the -World's popula- tion increases by leaps and bounds, this land, if suitable for other purposes, will inevitably be taken posscssion of—either by force or other- wise, by people who can make -better 116. e of it.

, . . Thus civilisation and the natural increase of population in our _modern piping times of peace have demanded that the aborigine shall no longer roam' over his native heath. The sad fate of the American Indian has proved that the character of a whole race cannot be changed, they cannot at once abandon the customs and traditions of their forefathers. In their case perhaps more than in that of any other race, has it been made clear, that the contact be- tween an unsophisticated people and one sup- posed to be on a higher plane of .civilisation is attended with disastrous results to tin/ abori- gine. Whether it is that the coloured man first picks up the white man's vices, whether it is , the vile quality of the trade gin and other liquor, or as has with more show of 'reason been urged —the adoption of clothing, .and consequence' in- crease of pulmonary disease, we do not propose to ilisellS9. But the fact remains that whenever the civilised white man has invaded in any large numbers a country with an aboriginal population, that population has almost invari- ably been gradually crushed out of existence. I Many instances will occur to the mind besides the North American Indians, the Tasmanians, the Australian :blacks, and the Maoris. But it is not only modern civilisation which has tended to crush aboriginal populations. The process has been a more or less constant one right down through the ages, as almost any period of ,history will demonstrate. It will pro- bably suffice for our purposes, however, if we take a few of the more prominent and remark. able examples.

CHILDREN OF THE PACE.. Turning to the probable cradle of the race-- Asia—where One finds nations which have—with the possible exception of Egypt—the oldest civi- lisations in the world, we find traces of still older races which havo been almost crushed out in the struggle for existence. Such, for instance. are the scanty remnants of the mysterious aborigines of Western Asia, Southern Arabia, and .Southern Persia. The indigenous black population of India, consisting of the Dravidian peoples of the Vindhya territory and of the b- ean, and probably most „nearly related to the Austral Negroes, display...an amazing vitality, and can hardly be said to he a dying race. rho Veddahs, the aborigines.of Ceylon, on the other hand, have dwindled in numbers to a few thou- sands only. This degraded hut interesting forest tribe Are said by ethnologists to be one of the lowest existing types of humanity. Their only attempt at clothing is a sort of apron of leaves. The polyglot populations of the Indo-China peninsula present something of a problem to the geienti6t. Not only have they been affected by alternate waves of Hindoo• and Chinese civilisation, but they also partake strongly of the character of the Malayan races which have played so promin- ent a part in the turbulent history of the penin- sula. That in the past the inhabitants attained . a high degree of civilisation is proved by the numerous ruined cities, notably the Buddhist cities of culture at Angkor Vath in Cambodia. Leaving the Asiatic mainland and pursuing our investigations further north we are natur- ally drawn ,to the land of the chrysanthemum and its interesting people. Ethnologi*4a t4liis that the Japanese are a mixed race, springing from Mongolian immigrants in prehistoric times and from an indigenous population. It is with the latter that we are more directly concerned. The Ainos, the aborigines of Japan are the un- kempt hunting and fisher folk still to be found in the island of Yezo. They are distinguished by their swarthy complexion and strong growth of hair.

EXTINCT WESTERN CIVILISATIONS.

One of the most astonishing races the world has ever seen was undoubtedly the Aztecs. They were not the original inhabitants of Mexicc—the aborigines were the Chichimecs, but their march of conquest culminated in the erection of the !State of Old Mexico and the evolution of a self- contained civilization which was the wonder of the New World, and only succumbed to the as- saults of Old World civilisation in the persons of Ithe Conquistadores. Their influence reached as ' far as Central America, where they met with the resistance of the civilized states of the Maya nations of Guatemala, Honduras, and Yucatan. Distinctly related to the Aztecs were the Pueblo tribes of the Western States of whom only a scattered remnant is left, living among the wonderful rock dwellings so eloquent of their former greatness. Not far away are the former haunts of the f ho- slione6, the Digger Indians of California, and the Yumas of. Arizona, now alas! as nations only shadows of their former selves. Further north still, on the bleak Pacific coast and forming the connecting link between the Esquimaux beyond the Arctic Circle and the lied Indians who roamed the prairies inland, are the Thlinkeets and Nutkas, the riaidas and the Chinooks, who get their living by fishing; and last mid lowest of all there is the degraded Hametz tribe—canni- bals who devour dead bodies. This is the home of the primitive mask, of belief in myths and legends and totems, of mnmmery and tattooing. But even these isolated and primitive tribes are losing many of their distinctive traits in their now more frequent contact with modern civiliza. tion. SOUTH AMERICAN INDIANS. • Another ancient civilization which forms a rmarkable parallel to that of the Mexican Aztecs, was the commonwmlth of the of Peru. Their identity is the more marked in that neither long survived the warlike methods of Western civilization as demonstrated by the Conquistadores. Strange to say neither of these. ancient states of the New World was aware of the other's existence. The "sun- kingdom" of the Incas presented a -unique study in social evolution. The social system was based on communistic principles - but the reign- ing dynasty of the: Incas was 'despotic in its rule. Nevertheless industry and agriculture were extremely flourishing, and this wonderful people attained a high level of culture and pro- ficiency in the arts. Their buildings and public works were such as to astonish the Spaniards at

THE MIDLOTHIAN CONTEST.

Above we give portraits Of the three candi- slates selected to do battle for Mr. Gladstone's old constituency. Mr. Alexander Shaw, the Liberal candidate, is the son of Lord Shaw, who was for some years an active member of the present Government. Be is an eloquent and persuasive speaker. Major J. A. Hope, whom the Unionists have chosen as their champion, is a popular figure in the district,

and is personally liked even by his opponents. This is his second attack on the seat, the Master of Elibank having defeated him in December, 1910. Provost R. Brown, the Labour candidate, is a very prominent figure among Labour leaders over the border, and the high municipal dignity to which he has attained is proof of the esteem in which he is held.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION. (N.A. CENTRE).

The conditions last week for anglers were of the worst possible kind, esrecially those who frequent the running streams Chester, Mickle Traffo-d and Hart- ford were the places to suffer the most. The waters at these plAces were running like a mill-race. A great improvement on Sunday had a tendency to draw several locals to wa,ers nearer home, after they had given angling up for the week-eud, and one or two walked the journey alter the train left. Ag,iiu the week passes without a specimen taking top place on the set boird and the only one to register was a gudgeon of loz. &inns. Several roach from , 8 z. to 103 z. were creeled by members, but knowing that one at 11, z. has been weighed in for the monthly " spt c al' there was little encouragement for them to visit headquarters.

CHESTER.

On or two members who visited the Dee and Canal bele had very poor returns. Otte member on trying tie river gave it up as unfishable, and crossed the town to the canal. HARTFORD.

Several visited the Weaver and Dane making the journey on Saturday et erring. Again disappointment met their tfforts, only the small fry falling to their skill. One member who visited the Weaver during the week and fished near the stone bridge, was doing well witu the perch much to the annoyance of several anglers who hail-from Liverpool who came within a yard of him and were throwing over his line. The member, who was alone, and i now getting isto the old school of anglers, was somewhat afraid of them, and the attitude they showed to him. Actions like this need very little comment to show their nusport- man-like nature and it was a great. pity thee wAre not More locals close at hand to demand of these " fish at any price " anglers, the fair play which every angler who visits here is entitled to.

NORTON.

The canal here had an impiovement both in number of members and bags of fish,the :ength between Norton Bridge and the waiehouses being the most popular sport. The members who did hest were Messrs Stout Potter, Dryers, Parnell, Moores, Hill, Dunbobbin, Wilsu end Hargreaves, jun. The weight would average from 2i11). to 31b. of fish, Hargreaves, jun., having four nice eels, besides hooking one he could not land, although he held it until assist ince came and then in the struggle that followed he got snatched up. W Wilson, who is only of a few weeks membership was also resocausible for a fine eel besides six or seven nice ruach. Moores, Hill and Dunbobbin tried the water on contest lines and had a fair day's sport. Br3ers was responsible for the biggest poach of the day with the exception of one taken by a non-member. This was caught between members, who were using the feeder, but the bole had been well baited before the angler bad dropped into it. If this roach had be-n taken by a member the set board would have been topped with it by this season's roach horn Norton.

NOTIcE. The motor has again been chartered fer the annual match and a list will be ptt up at the club holse for members to put down their names if they with to travel by it, or they may hand in their names to any official and they will be booked up. Any member putting down his name or handing it to an official will be asked to pay whether he goes by it or not, as we hare to guarantee the requited number. Please note: Members must be fully paid up or they will not be allowed to fish the match.

ELLESMERE,.

A member who was on holiday here reports that fishing at this spot has not been up to expectation, in fact the natives sty it has never been caught at its usual form this season. One or two nice roach and perch were cree ed, but not in the quantities they usually take them. The roach were 3oz. and lib 4oz. respectively and the perch taken by a Wigan angler Jib 10oz. The Mere is a fine fishery when they are on feed, and any member requiring information with regard to lodginns, hire of boats and where to fish, cau have same by inquiring from the secretary.—AS.

Wycliffe Congregationaj Church.

• On Sunday lust the pulpit was occupied by the Rev M F Bovenizer, of Liverpool. In the morning the adaress to the children was based on the love which parents devote to them, and the preacher pointed out that the debt to frther and mother could never be repaid with money, flit only by ohedieuce. The retmon was from John x, 9," I am the door ; by me if any man enter in he shall be saved, and shall go in, and go out, and find pastare." The supremacy of Christ's claims was emphasized," lam the way," lam the truth," "11 am the life." Christ taught that life is continuous' and ever advancing, and the only evidence that we are alive is that we are growing, and are not content to cling to customs ADA doctrines for their own sakes. We should have the courage of our fifth, and not be afraid to go forward. • In the evening the preacher bared his discourse on the words " we must work the works of Him that sent me." (John ix. 4.1' The remarkable thing about Jesus be said, was the passion with which He welcomed fellow workers, and the triendship of these men with Christ resulted in them becoming the emancipators of hummit.Y. Our commercial progress and out commer- cial prosperity had multiplied the problems that score crying to thd churches for solution, but sometimes the churches did not hear the cry. If the churches did not lead and inspire, who was to do it? The preacher pro- ceeded to show that if they linked themselves with Christ they would be possessed of a great impulse and a great inspiration. As an example, he referred to the great work of General Booth and pleaded for the dedication of all Christians to work for Christ.

OF WEEK-END NEWSPAPERS COMPRISES THE WARRINGTON EXAMINER, published every Saturday morning, the principal and oldest weekly paper for Warrington, Stockton Heath, Penketh, Winwick, Padgate, Lymm, Ste., being one of the largest and best weekly papers in Lancashire and Cheshire. The district covered by this paper comprises a large trading and agricultural tract in South Lancashire and Mid - Cheshire.

Boy Scouts.

,

On Thursday evening the Officers and Scouts of the let Warrington Troop attended the Park Picture Palace at the kind invitation of their Hon. Scoutmaster (Mr J B Starkey), who is also the chairman of the local association. The Scouts paraded in full force at the Fair Ground, Queen-street, where they were joined by the let Orford Troop. The parade was under the command of Scoutmaster Bennett. On arrival at the Park Picture Palace the boys were welcomed by Mr J B Starkey, Mr C J Newton (local secretary) and Mr I R Bibby, the Palace manager. Special pictures were shown illustrating interesting periods in the life of a Boy Scout such as a dispatch running, parade and inspection at Chatsworth House, first aid and tea time. Pictures of the local Scouts were shown in camp at Delamere, Kophold Farm and Victoria Park, and caused much surprise and amusement. Great interest was shown in the picture of the "Boy Scouts' Funeral " of eight of the victims of the terrible calamity off the Isle of Sheppey on August Sunday. , Just as the coffins were being lowered into the grave on the pictures the "Last Post" was sounded by Scout T Herlev of the Ist Weston Point Troop and one of the local scouts. This caused quite a stir and added a realistic touch to the very pathetic picture. Several comic pictures were shown and, afterwards the Scouts marched through the town to the fair ground, where they were dismissed after having passed a very enjoy- able and instructive evening.

SIR EDWARD GREY has A BRITISH not delayed in meeting PROTEST. the situation created by the passing into law of the Panama Canal Act with the formal protest which people of all political com- plexions in this country expected. The British Charge d'Affaires at Washington, acting in the absence of Mr BRYCE on instructions from Downing-street, has intimated that his Majesty's Government intend, when they have had time to make a full examination of the Act and of the Memorandum of President TAFT3O make a more extended representation of their views, and that they reserve the right to submit ;the question Ito arbitration under the terms of the existing Anglo-American Arbitration Treaty.:No less prompt and determined action could have been justified. In the Hay-Pauncefote Treaty the British and the United States Governments entered into a definite and well-understood bargain. This country surrendered certain undoubted rights in regard to the construction of the Panama Canal, receiving in return the under- taking sealed and signed that the rights of the shipping of all nations to enter the Canal on absolutely equal terms would be estab- lished. We need not repeat here the clause in the Treaty which bears upon this point. In the understanding of all impartial persons it cannot be interpreted otherwise than as a solemn and unqualified declaration that there shall be no discrimination in charges and dues in favour of any nation. The representa- tions which have been or will be made to Washington are entirely friendly in tone,and is satisfactory that what has been said on this side of the Atlantic in the Press and public comment has been uniformly and studiously moderate and frees from provo- cative language. What has been said in violence on the question of the Act has appeared in American journals of varying shades of opinion and of great influence.

But the moderation of language, and the sincere desire which is evident on all sides in this country that this question should be discussed and settled with no straining of the close friendship between the Governments and the peoples of Great Britain and the United States, must not be taken as implying weakness in the protest which will be made. Happily, the machinery for amicable settlement stands ready to hand. Under the Arbitration Treaty of 1908 we can ask that the matter should be referred to The Hague Tribunal, and there is no reason to suppose that Mr. TAFT, from whose lips came the suggestion of a more extensive Arbitration Treaty, will offer any opposition to that course. It is even possible that the necessity for that request may not arise. Between now and the autumn the British Government will have the oppor- tunity to consider the whole question in all its bearings, and it is possible that negotia- tions which will then be opened will result in a happy conclusion of the difference. Meanwhile, the counsel of sobriety must be respected. In Mr. TAFT'S Memorandum to Congress, the text of which is now available, the President points out that he was induced to sign the Act despite the protest from the British Government because of the necessity for the enactment of the provisions of the Act as to the maintenance and government of the Canal.

That, of course, is perfectly intelligible, and it is clear from what follows that the

PRESIDENT does not intend that his act & prejudice the consideration of the iniportant representations which have been made. protest from a friendly Government," be the. declares, "supported as it is bY expressed views of distinguished Members ot both Houses,invites the greatest care and the ghts closest examination on our part of our under our treaties, with a view to preserving national honour and observing our solemn obligations." Distinguished American lawyers in Congress and Senate have taken the view that the exemption of the American, coastwise shipping from the dues which wilt fall upon all other shipping will be a crimination in favour of the United Ste ' That is the position of the British tes' Go G ment, and we cannot see how declaration on the part of the America°, Legislature that the Treaty is not violatea can affect the situation. Mr. Taft adds to the resolution affirming this declara" tion, which he desires the Senate and Ceti- gress to pass, a statement that Pert: aggrieved can appeal to the United Ste'f Courts, and he cites this as "an earnest good faith in attempting to keep our Treat/ obligations," It is obviously impossible fa the local Courts of the United States t° be, set up as the interpreters of International rights in this way. All these matters, lie'r ever, will be treated fully in the comlfl-01; tions which will shortly pass between t two Governments, and meantime it is satl'' factory to know that on both sides there 15., the fullest desire that the whole discussie' shall be carried on without asperity.

If the Tory complaint is that the oPeration of the Act should have been post- Aoned, it must be pointed out that the Lords could have rejected it as it was not sent to them under the provisions of the Parliament Act. It is often suggested, and not withou reason, that the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee is a funny Committee. Alder- man Bolton and his fellow members have a distinctive way of doing things. For instance, we note from the minutes issued for consideration at Tuesday's Council meet- ing that the Committee has first decided to repaint and repair the Old Warps and then ask the Borough Surveyor Lr an estimate of the cost. A truly Hibernian mode of pro- cedure!

The Board of Trade -has issued a notice to masters of vessels, drawing attention to the recommendation of Lord Mersey in the Titanic report that the attention of ship- masters should be called to the fact that it is a misdemeanour not to go to the relief of a vessel in distress, when possible to do so. The Board lay stress on the point that it has always been the duty of masters to proceed to the assistance of vessels in distress, and this duty exists apart from statutory obliga- tions. * * *

The Duke of Norfolk. has committed to Lord Newton the task of writing a memoir of Lord Lyons. The choice is a happy one, for Lord Newton, who nowadays enlivens the House of Lords debates with his caustic wit, served under Lord Lyons for some years in the Paris Embassy. The memoir should be a fascinating one, for Lord Lyons, apart from his earlier experiences, was in Paris during the twenty most momentous years of French modern history. He saw the fading glories of the Second Empire, and its fall. * *

* - Can our penetrating observations on the Smoke Nuis ince already have moved the powers that be to spirited action? At any rate the Health Committee has passed a resolution ordering the Town Clerk to lake proceedings against a frequently warned offender. " Wl,ere there is smoke there is fire" is an adage which may be disproved in this instance. We shall believe in the reality of the prosecution when the defendant com- pany appears in Court. There are so many ways of avoiding these unpleasantnesses. * *

We notice that at the Conservative trip to New Brighton on Saturday, Mr. E. A. White nobly endeavoured to make up for the depressing conditions by a free employment of his joking facilities. He remarked that "the country was suffering from two kinds of bad things at present—bad weather and bad Government." He further expressed an admirable faith that both would change. We are inclined to agree with him, but we don't think the weather will change soon enough to save the farmer from ruin; neither do we think the Government will change yet 1 ' awhile—or for the better when it does change Of what illustrious members of his own party would Mr. White form a Government? * * * _ .

There is a curious parallel to the misfor- tune under which Norwich is suffering just now in the annals of Queen Elizabeth. In August 1578 the Queen visited Norwich in State, and a great pageant was prepared in her honour. Among other things a number of nymphs were concealed in an improvised cave and were to come out and greet the Queen as she passed. Unfortunately at the very moment when the Queen passed in her coach, a thunder shower came down like a waterspout and almost drowned the water nymphs, while awful peals of thunder drowned the singing. * * *

Some amusing stories are to be found, as might be expected, in the volume of rominiscences, " May I Tell you a Story ?" which has been published by that entertain- ing story-teller, Miss Helen Mar. One concerns the rich American who, having ordered a Venus de Milo from Europe, was extremely annoycd to receive a statue with mutilated arms, and promptly sued the Express Company for damages—and got them ! :diss Mar adds as a foot-note that after telling this story in London once a member of the audience inquired of her afterwards, "But if the company had smashed the goods, why shouldn't they be liable ?" *

John Wesley would probably have pro- claimed the disastrous floods at Norwich to be a Divine judgment upon the inhabitants, whom he cordially detested. His " Journal" is full of lamentations at the " perverseness" and " fickleness " of the people. When he preached there in 1759 the congregation was rude and noisy." In 1761 he preached at the cathedral, but "Bedlam broke out among the hearers." He found" no people in all England or Ireland" so hopeless from a religious point of view by reason of their "unstable character" and "marvellous ignorance." Wesley did not think the Norwich men improved upon acquaintance. for in 1785 he declared the citizens to be " the most ficklend yet the most stubborn" he had ever met.

Abney Park,where to-day General Booth's remains are laid to rest, was the property of General Charles Fleetwood, one of the most honest and public-spirited of Cromwell's lieutenants, and a local tradition clings to the belief that Cromwell's bones lie in the Park. The place passed into the hands of Sir Thomas Abney, Lord Mayor of London, in 1700, and he built himself a house there. After his daughter's death the house was used for ' many years as d Wesleyan College, but, the park and grounds having been made into a cemetery, the house was pulled down in 1845.

. . We would elicit the sympathies of our readers for the sad case of Miss Irene La wley daughter of the late Lord Wenlock. This young lady has written to her tenants at Escrick Park, telling them that she is most unfortunately prevented from living among them and maintaining the property because of the death duties. She adds: "I cannot tell you how sorry I am that this should be the case." This poor young lady's father died in January last, and she has had to pay death duties amounting to about £67,000. This leaves her an estate of the paltry gross value of £452,000. It is not to be expected that she should do her duty by her tenants under such impoverished circumstances, is it? *4* _ _ _

The latest pen-portrait of Mr. Lloyd George comes from the Vicar of Cherton, Devizes, the Rev. T. S. Cunningham, who writes as follows in the following courteous and Christian-like terms: It is time to take off the gloves and talk stiaight. I make b-id to tell you that with all his cleverness the Chancellor of the Exchequer is a super-monkey. He has all the characteristics of the monkey finely developed. He has its mischief; its quickness; he strokes your face with a patron- ising air and bites your nose when you are not looking. He possesses the honesty of the horsecouper, and a love for truth which is prominebt in the character cf Ananias. Mr. Lloyd George has not only four aces in the pack ; he has 414 if he wants thorn.. There is no bribe too mean, no lie too monstrous, but he will cram it down the throats of the electors who are fools enough to believe him. We should imagine that when the Vicar took his gloves off he stuck his hands in mud !

LOCAL PARS.

CNCIISt and Taxi. A cyclist fell foul of a taxi-cab in Knutsford-road, on Monday evening. Thomas Maskell, 19, Barry-street, Warrington, was cycling along the road on Monday morning, when he failed to observe the approach of the taxi-cab, the splashboard of which struck him. Maskell was knocked off his machine and sustained a few scratches on the hands, but no serious injury. His cycle was badly damaged. Motor Accident at Grappenhall. Another motor collision occurred at the dangerous corner by the Dog and Dart Inn on Sunday afternoon. About 4.15 a motor car belonging to Mr. W. R. Smith, of Chorlton-cum-Hardy, was travelling in the direction of Warrington from 'Manchester, when it collided with a car belonging to Mr. Lees, Alexandra-street, \Val- rington. Mr. Lees was rendered unconscious and was attended by Dr. Jago. He was afterwards taken home in a taxi-cab. Both cars were badly damaged Presentation to a Sailor. The presentation by the Warrington Women's Liberal Association to Samuel Ashworth, the sailor who made such a gallant rescue at Latchford Locks on the occasion of the annual picnic of the association, will take place at the Liberal Club on Tuesday even- ing. A handsome sum was subscribed, and Ashworth will receive an inlaid mahogany full chime clot k and a pair of fine bronze side ornaments. A suitable inscription adorns the clock, which is on view in the window of the Grand Clothing Hall, Bridge Street.

. _ Palace and Hippodrome "Star " Engagement. America's greatest and most versatile artiste Charles T Aldrich heads the bill for next week. Aldrich had the honour of appearing before the King and Queen at the coinmand performance in July, and is said to be unequalled in his own style of humour. His visit to Warrington should be taken advantage of by all. Other artistes who will appear are: Thornbury, lightning painter and entertainer; Doris and Frances, simultaneous dancers; The Woodwards, cycling balancing act; May Poulton, vocal comedienne; The Three Singing Colliers ; and the Yorkshire rustic, Jack Lane, and his piano. COW Impaled. A exciting incident occurred in the streets of Warrington on Monday morning when a Kerry cow belonging to Sir Gilbert Greenall ran amok. The beast was being removed from Walton Hall to Bank Quay Station to be entrained for Lostock Gralam, when it escaped. It was re-captured in the Town Hall Yard, but broke away again when the keeper, William Anderton, tried to put it in a float. The animal rushed madly tilt ough the town and when near the Causeway Hotel it attemnted to jump the iron railings surrounding an adjoining field and in doing so became impaled on a six-inch spike which was firmly lodged in the chest of the animal. Workmen came and took a section of the railings down, the animal raving all the time.

Warrington Tram Driver Goes to Australia. A presentation was made on Monday at noon to Mr. J. Brailey (who is going to Australia) a tram driver in the employ of the Corporation. The gift, which took the form of a travelling trunk, was handed over to Mr Brailey in the mess room of the Corpora- tion Tramways Department. It had been subscribed to by the employes of the car sheds and the permanent way. DEATH OF MRS. R. GARNETT.

We regret to announce the death at an eally hour yesterday morning of Mrs. Robert Garnet!, Ball Nook, Penketh. The deceased lady was thought to have recovered from an indisposition in the bummer, and during the present week she had been out and about the village of Penketh looking very much stronger and better. On Tuesday last she was at Southport for the day, and seemed as well as ever on ieturning home. She complained of pain on Wednesday morning and gradually became unconscious, finally passing away in her sleep yesterday morning. She had always enjoyed good health and was certainly one of the most familiar and respected figures in the Penketh and Warrington district.

Mrs. Garnett was born at Stanwix. Carlisle. in 1834, and was married to the late Mr. Robert Garnett, of Penketh, Warrington, in 1859. Since then she has lived at Hall Nook, Penketh. Her ,married life with Mr. Garnett embraced 44 years of happy work in Penketh and Warrington. Since Mr. Garnett died in 1903 she has lived happily surrounded by her children and grand.?hildren. She has all her life been a devoted worker in the temperance cause. She was also a member of Warrington Literary and Philo- sophical Society, and Manchester Field Naturalists Society. All woik in the village to encourage horticulture and cottage gardening received her warm support. She bad travelled very largely in Italy, Switzerland, Egypt and the Holy Land and written many descriptive articles on her tours, and lectured in many places on the scenes she has visited. Mrs. Garnett's devotion to the Wesleyan Church at Penketh had always been a part of her innermost life and nowhere will she be more keenly missed than among the workers of the Wesleyan body. One of her great friends was General Booth of the Salvation Army who bad been called to his last ',home a week earlier. They corresponded regularly and the General frequ!htly visited her at Penketh. She tmolt an untiring interest in all the work of the Salvation Army, which only came second to her love for her own church.

The funeral will take place at Sankey churchyard on Monday at 3.30, being preceded by a service at Penketh Wesleyan Church at 2.30.

FUNERAL OF GENERAL BOOTH.

Homage of Army " and the Nation.

General Booth was laid-to rest on Thursday. Five thousand of his offleers and soldiers—in 51 brigades—marched in a mile-long pageant from the Victoria Embankment to the graveside, and many thousands who are not members of the " Army " joined in the procession from Shoreditch to Abbey Park Cemetery. Scenes and incidents of stirring emotion, with which the burials of Kings and Princes in recent times bear no comparison, were witnessed by the open grave, round which the Army's forces were massen for the last rites, tributes and thanksgivings. Members of the dead General's family, struggling with tears, spoke their praise of a great leader and their love for a good father. Outside the Cemetery (writes the "Daily Chronicle" correspondent) stretching away through a world of mean streets, there was an immense multitude, which surged up to the gates. They were not noisy as individuals, but these moving masses of humanity made a dull, muffled uproar Inside, there was a spacious lor eliness and a great peace. His grave was ready for him—a deep and narrow vault, above which a high platform had been built. I stood looking down into that grave for a moment and savv how tte flowers werebloomilag above the tomb of the Army's mother and the General's wife. Busy bands were at work making a bower of the broad platform above, bringing inrumerable wreaths,'sent as tributes from the great people of the world, and, more beautiful still, poses githered from cottage garde%s and sent by the children of poverty who knew the General as their friend.

We had two hours of waiting, when. at 3 o'clock WP saw far away between the trees, the glint and fire of the first banners. It ass the vanguard of the great army which was brirging the body of the dead Chief. I stool on the high platform I olting down upon those long battalions aa they p ,ured in a great tide helow me, flowing round to an open hoarded space facing the empty grave. It was an unforgetable sight, not of pageantry, but of a great living impulse dominat- ing this stream of hmitan bei. gs They were tired— spent—with march ng. Many of the older men and women c ,uld hardly walk to their halting place. Younger men and women supp u-ted them, but upon the faces of yt.u,ir and old ale there was the look of an immense faticue. These drooping figures, these staggering regiments had endured a great physical ordeal. But they were cheerful stid, strangely and wonderfully gay. Here were laughing eyes, eyes that burned wi.h an exultation of spirit, humble rugged faces transfigured a little by the fervour of enthusiasm. It seemed that they had come not for a burying, but for a love feast.

For a little while the songs of the Army went thrilling tbruut,th the great graveyard with that martial spi it of joyous hope which ch.racterises so many of the Salvation Army hymns. But presently General Bramwell Both rose and walked slowly to the rail whicli overlooked his legions, and, beyond, the people of the neighbourhood crowded on roofs and walls.

The now General's oration over the body of his father was noble in its siucerily, profound in its emotion. Many times be was silent because tears choked his voice. Many times he spoke as he wept, with a courage overmastering his grief. The very incoherence of some of his sentences was of haunting pathos, as when he suddenly stopped and then cried out in a loud voice, "He was a grand old man !" and as when he spoke of his own love for the man who was not only his general but hia dear father and friend, and as when, many times, he turned and stretched his hand out to the coffin, and said, " Here lies a man who loved you," or" By this open grave I speak to you of that great spirit." And as officer after officer rose to bear testimony to his dead chief, to praise his great achievements, the same note was sounded again and again. Panegyric faltered away into personal devotion. SOLO IHAT WASN'T SUNG.

The General's youngest daughter, Mrs. Booth- Hellberg, was to have sung a solo. But when she rose with the book and came forward and faced the people her voice failed her, and she just wept before them. Presently she spoke little tearful sentences. "I would like to sing to you lust before he died the General stretched out his hand to tAke mine. . . .

He could not see it, but when he grasped it be said, 'Sing to me.' So I sang this song to him I cannot sing it now, dear people. . . You will forgive me? Will you sing it for me ?" So the people sang. an old familiar hymn. but it was all a failure, a beautiful failure, because they were crying too. GENERAL BOOTH'S WILL. Estate to Which the Army Contributed Nothing. _

The following is contained in a summary of the late General. Booth's testamentary depositions, which was officially issued on 'l2buri day :- 1. By his will, which deals only with the properties held by him as General of the Salvation Army or on any like public trust, he formally vests all such properties in his successor as the General of the Army, to be held by him on trust. 2. By three codicils be deals with his small private property, the net value of which amounts to £487 19s. his he gives to the Salvation Army, with the excep- tion of certain private papers and memoranda, which he leaves to his eldest SOD, the present General ; and a few articles, chosen by himself, which he gives as mementoes to each of his children and children-in-law.

By the codicils he also deals with property of the present estimated value of £5,295, representing moneys which, as is well known, were many years ago settled by the late Henry Reed on the late General for his private use. It was this provision which enabled the late General throughout his life to draw no remuneration of any kind from the Salvation Army. The late General divides this settled property among his children, Mr. Bramwell Booth—his successor— Catherine, Marian, Herbert, Eva and Lucy. The General appoints his successor as his executor.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The Greatest Scoundrel Living." The large degree of success which attended the pro- duction of Mr. A. M. Loaders' seusational orania "The Greatest Scoundrel Living" by Mr. and Mrs. Lodge— Percy's Co., in February last has been repealed at the Royal Court Theatre during the week when a con- densed version of this now popular play has been pre- sented twice nightly. Incredible, as it may seem the production has lost little or none of its sensa- tionalism, and the other leading characteristics of the piece are well maintained. It is one of those captivat- ing dramas bristling with exciting incidents and hair raising situations, and the comedy is of such a light aid palatable character as to give just the necessary relief from the heavier aspects. A feeling story of love, hatred, murder and revenge is rapidly unfolded before the audience, who are gripped from the first by the clever acting and true representation of the characters by the various members of the company. Mr. Lodge Percy distinguishes himself as the sailor hero, Frank Fielding, and infus.s real life i.to the part with his Inilliant acting. Mr. Graham Robetts is at once a success as the scoundrel,Percival Haynes. He is the typical adventurer and is fully rewarded by a more than usual quota of hisses from the" popular" parts of the house. Miss Ethel Ramsay soon earns the appreciation and sympathy of the audience by the sincere way in which she plays the part of Rosa Moulton, the *affianced wife of the hero. Miss Kathleen Granston, as Mabel Cross, the foil of Baynes, shares equally the honours. The comedy, which is an outstanding feature of the production,is in capable hands. As Sam Grub, the rustic poet, Mr. • Will Kirk brings the house down with the humorous poems srhich he inflicts on a charming house- maid on whom he has amorous designs. As his rival for the hand of the fair damsel, Mr Ernest A Whitmee, as Tom Masters, keeps the ball rolling in a clever manner, whilst the part of Bessie Bright, the housemaid, is in the happy charge of Miss Amy Shaw.. The other parts are wed sustained and the setting is perfect, the cave scene being especially well done. 'the houses which have assembled nightly have been amply satisfisd with the excellence of the whole production.

"PROSPERITY WHICH BAFFLES' DESCRIPTION."

MR. HAROLD SMITH'S CHICKENS

COME HOME.

THE DEAR FOOD OUTCRY IN GERMANY.

When Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., became a Tariff Reform tripper at Whitsuntide he spent a week-end (or thereabouts) in Germany. This brief visit was quite long enough for him to discover that Paradise was within easy reach of Warrington, and apparently suffered nothing from being ruled by the Kaiser. He promptly "wrote home" about it, and our contemporary the " Guardian " published a particularly telling extract from the Member's letter. The extract was as follows :

I wish all Warrington could come and see the happiness and prosperity of the people. It baffles description. A week or two later we published a number offacts relating to the conditions of existence among the German people which Mr. Harold had apparently overlooked during his short visit. Some of these facts had reference to work and wages, the prices of bread and meat, and the consumption 'of horse-flesh and dog-flesh by the very people whose happiness and prosperity "baffle description:" but ne'er a word replied he. To-day there are further facts disclosed for his delectation by the Berlin correspondent of the Conservative" Morning Post." 'Tele- graphing last Friday night this correspond- ent—who may be presumed to know rather more about the "happiness and prosperity" of the German people than Mr Harold Smith —writes as follows : The serious attention •;) all classes of the population is being directed to THE RAPID INCREASE IN THE PRICE OF MEAT

of all sorts. It is not only that pork, the staple flesh food of the poor, mounts in price, but veal, mutton, and beef in all their shapes are apparently similarly affected. The general feeling is that the period of high prices has only begun, and that the coming autumn and winter will see prices which will make

THE CONSUMPTION OF THE BETTER KINDS OF FLESH PRACTICALLY IMPOSSIBLE

for the families of the working classes. According to statistics published at Stuttgart from official sources, the increase in price during the last decade has been

NEARLY 40 PER CENT. and the document from which this fact is taken declares that never have prices for meat reached their present height. It is a poor comfort to a man with limited means to hear that this rapid rise is an inter- national phenomenon, and that in Vienna, Budapest, Paris, and Rotterdam prices have risen correspondingly. From many parts of the country news comes of meet- ings passing energetic resolutions

CALLING ON THE GOVERNMENT to open their frontiers or at least to repeal some of the more drastic regulations deal- ing with the importation of meat and live stock, and in several populous centres— for example, Breslau—the butchers have issued a manifesto stating that the rise in prices which has set in, and which is sure to continue for the next six months, is not due to any steps taken by them but to the impotence of German agriculturists,whose flesh production has not kept pace with the increase of the population. It is not only the prices of meat which have risen so ominously. SIMILAR COMPLAINTS are heard about vegetables, especially potatoes, another staple dish of the poor. Milk is also rising in price. According to official figures which have appeared here the average weekly expendi- ture of a family of four persons of the middle classes on food has risen from 2350 marks in January, 1911, to 26'10 marks in

July of this year. The words are those of the "Morning Post's" Berlin correspondent, but the head- ings are ours. We put them in so that they might catch the eye of "all Warrington" before accepting the Member's invitation to come and see that happiness and prosperity of the German people which "baffles description."

===== 0002601_19120831_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE AND MORE HORSEFLESH.

- The " Munich " correspondent ofthe "Daily News" has also sent a striking report on the enormous increase in the cost° of meat in Germany. He says : In North Germany the position is if any- thing still worse. As the official Govern- ment organ,the "Norddeutsche Allegemeine Zeitung," points out, the consumption of horseflesh is increasing rapidly For Berlin alone 5,924 horses were slaughtered for food during the first six months of the pre- sent year as compared with 4.461 during the same period a year ago. The month of July showed another increase, and the present month promises a figure over 1,000 —a record for August. During the last few months from 20 to 30 new centres for the sale of horseflesh have been opened in the working-class quarters of Berlin. The Radical " Berliner Tageblatt " is bitterly sarcastic over this RECORD IN HORSEFLESH CONSUMPTION for the capital city of the Empire. It suggests that very soon a record in the consumption of rat flesh will also be reached. Already, it says, the country is suffering from underfeeding. The consumption of meat is a good deal below the amount set by the Imperial Health Office as necessary for proper nourishment. The Member for Warrington might write a special article on •' How to be happy and Hungry " ; by an English M.P. It would doubtless be eagerly accepted by the German press and read with avidity by the German Ipeople.

A controversy is still merrily proceeding as to the opening of cinematograph shows on Sunday. Theatre and music hall pro- prietors are demanding that, unless these, shows are closed, they must have permission to open their houses seven days a week, a proposition to which actors and musicians are unalterably opposed. It is urged that the whole tone and atmosphere of picture palaces are vastly different from theatres and music halls, and that they are a potent means of attracting men and women from the public house. But there is another and very large section who vigorously object to any further secularisa- tion of the British Sunday. Some go so far as to oppose the use of trams, and to decline to receive letters, cook dinners, or do anything involving labour on the Sabbath. It is unnecessary,however,to be rigid gabbatarians of this kind in order to deprecate on economic and social, no less than moral and religious grounds, the continual encroachments made on the weekly day of rest. The tendency to indulge in pleasure and to add to the amount of work done on Sunday is increasing. It is not a hopeful symptom of the national char acter, and workers, above all, should resist it, and make a vigorous stand for one day's rest in seven.

WARRINGTON BEAT THE LEAGUE

CHAMPIONS.

A large number of spectators assembled in the Wnrringtou Corp,,ration Baths, Legh-street, on Thursday evening, when the chief attracti 311 was the League match in os,nnection with the third division of the Liverpool and District Water Plo and Squadron League. THE POLO MATCH. HOLYHEAD AND 9REENORE7NEW 2:ONDON

AND NORTH-WESrERN STEAMER.

Once again the Loudon and North-Western Railway Co. have placed themselves in the very front rank of those who provide for the comfort of their cross- channel passengers. They have just had delivered to them the new 21-knot turbine steamer " Greenore " bull' by Nless,s Cammell, Laird, and Co., Ltd., Birken- head,for the C ,co pany's Holyhead and Greenore service. The new vessel is a turbine steamer with water-tube boilers of the Admiralty type, and during her trials between Holyhead and Greenore, she attained a speed of 21 knots. A noteworthy feature of the vessel is the total absence of vibration, either when running at full or slow speeds. The following is is description of the 1" Greenore " :—Her principal dimensions are—Length, between perpendiculars, 305 feet • breadth, moulded, I 40 feet : depth.moulded to bridge deck, 23 feet 3 inches, The vessel is built to Lloyd's special class and to to Blard of Trade survey, and has been designed to meet the general requirements of the Company for the Irish Channel service. The speed of the vessel with her full dead weight on board is 21 knots. Sleeping accommodation is pro- vided for about 176 first-class passengers in staterooms, and the ladies' cabins, the cabins being fitted out in a special manner. In addition to the ordinary accommo- dation of the vessel, there are two cabins cle fu.ra which may be reserved specially for passengers at a charge of 10i for the trip. Tnese cabins are fitted with a very consfurtable single bedstead, writing table, easy chairs sofa, portable electric lamp, Ac, and are, in fact, as luxurious as the cabins on any ocean liner. These cabins are sure to prove a great attraction to motorists using this route. Passengers who have reserved these Icabins will be allowed to embark at Holyhead at 9.30 p.m. and remain on board the steamer nest morning at Greenore until 9.30 a,m.

PUBLISIIE.D BY SPECIAL ARRANGEMENT

rHE ROMANCE OF DRUGS SAND POISONS.

[COPYRIGHT.]

There is an almost terrifying fascination to some minds about the awful potentialities con- tained even in the most common-place forms of ; the blue bottle, with its warning label of red. The drop of laudanum for the troublesome tooth, the salts of lemon to take out a stain, the cyanide of potassium, one of the most deadly ; drugs obtainable, which is used regularly by ; photographers, and not always kept as safely as is desirable, even the household ammonia, so ; often left carelessly about in unlabelled bottles, ; all have as element of horror about them, when ; regarded as the possible means of taking human life. The wonder of modern times is not that there do occur cases of accidental and inten- tional poisoning, but that they do not occur more frequently. There is a general idea that the sale of poisons is carefully restricted; but the precaution stops at a formal regulation that every purchaser gives some 'name or address, not invariably his own, for each consignment, and it would be easy, in a short round of chemist's shops, for a determined poisoner to obtain sufficient material to' poison a whole town.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A DYING " ART!'

Fortunately, however, for modern times, with the facilities, for obtaining deadly drugs, and their immense variety, poisoning as a fine art has gone out of fashion, and the majority of cases that come to pass are either ones of bungling self-murder or of careless ignorance. It is fatally easy to take salts of lemon for Epsom salts, if they are left about unmarked; but an amazing rapidity of swallowing, or slowness of taste, must account for those thirsty souls who :drink up the contents of a beer-bottle filled :with ammonia. There are few crimes more horrible than that of intentional poisoning. It is more cold- blooded, more treacherous and systematic than any other form of murder; it must require a stronger nerve, a harder heart, a more vindic- tive hatred, or .a more unrelenting cupidity for the motive that induces it, than any other v•l- -lainy on record. And is it not a strange thing that in contemplating the names that have come down from the ages, black with the hatred and loathing of their contemporaries and of pos- terity, among the lists of poisoners the names of women are in the proportion of three to two. Poison is the most cowardly and cruel of weapons. It is the instrument of 'black treachery, and is very often administered in the sacred name of hospitality. It has been asserted that woman, once "screwed up to the sticking-point," can keep her courage there longer than a man; of which theory Lady Mac- beth is an example. Perhaps this accounts some- thing for woman's pre-eminence in the poisonef's art. But more, probably, lies in the morbid fascination which the drugs have upon them, and which, if once indulged, sometime grows to a mania. The secrecy and stealth fos- ter the germ of incipient insanity, and the woman-poisoner seems to become a very relent- less fiend, with all a lunatic's cunniig and re- sourcefulness. •

In the Middle Ages there still lingered a kind of glamour about the art of poisoning, a refine- ment -which gave it a vague respectability, lift- ing it out of the plane of the common run of bloody deeds and daylight stabbing', hich barely evoked comment in those stirring days. The days were not far remote when the com- pulsory or voluntary draught of hemlock Ivas considered an honourable end to a tangled life. and it was an easy transition of a topsy-turvy age, to transfer the taking of the fatal dose to an outsider who threatened to interfere with one's own interests.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BALEFUL 330EGIAS.

There is a tendency of late days to deal a tardy tribute to the scant merits of the family whose great name has passed down the ages as the most infamous of poisoners and assassins. Murder must have been in the blood of the rapacious Borgias, who did to death without the slightest compunction, any who came be- tween them and their lusts or greed. Church- men whose riches they coveted, husbands who came between a vile woman and a new amour: elder brothers who stood in the way of a heritage, all were held fair game for the magic "white powder of the Borgias," whiCh wrought such havoc among the first families of Vome. The poisoning of his young hostage, the Indian Prince Djeum, for a bribe, was One of the least of the achievements of the monstrous Alexan- der, whose foul deeds of treachery were only surpassed by his worthy children, Cmsar and Lucrezia. A reign of terror pervaded the state, where every citizen of any standing or property might expect either poison or assassi- nation, and the confiscation of his belongings. The physician of the Lateran hospital was paid well for poisoning such rich patients as name under his charge; and Ca,sar Borgia employed a small force of women to deal poison to children they were paid to nurse.

The moat rapacious and powerful of the Car- dinals was marked out for their prey, and fell a victim to Borgia hospitality. "his body," so ran the pasquinade. " went to earth, his soul to hell, his goods to the Pope." Within a week, three cardinals succumbed, and their property was annexed, while society looked on, aghast and helpless. Poetic justice visited the revolt- ing Pope, though the lesson did Caesar Borgia very little good. The rich Cardinal Hadrian had been bidden to a supper-party in a vineyard of the Vatican. and Lucrezia herself prepared the vintage in his honour. With what qualms the summons was obeyed, we may well imagine. Whether by design Qr accident, the flagoni were misplaced upon the table. Lucrezia her- self handed the harmless flagon to the guest, I and Alexander and his son drank of the pre- pared wine. In six days the hated Pope was dead, and there was general rejoicing in the momentary forgetfulness that a worse tyrant remained. The ghastly spectacle of the Pope's discoloured corpse so terrified\ the servants that none could be found to approach it; and the man before whom all Rome had trembled was followed to hie grave by a solitary mourner—a black dog, which, men said, was the devil. The younger Borgias after the Pope's death administered the "white powder" more freely and recklessly than ever: some maggot of in- sanity must surely have infested their brains, for in other respects Casar Borgia earned th€ approval of Machiavelli as being the ablest statesman of his day. LADY POISONERS.

From the fabled wedding gloves by which " ice, hearted " Catherine de Medici is said to have compassed the death of the Queen ilegentrand her jewelled fans, whose poisoned sprint; tore the fingers of those who opened them, French *omen have shown a strong penchant for the science of secret, poisoning. The abominable teaching of Sainte Croix let loose upon European society a sprinkling of abandoned women who had at theii finger-ends a whole art and science of deadly drugs and potions. The two fortune-telling adventurers, Lavoisier and Laving,court, in their time executed more irregular dooms-than did the public executioner in the official discharge of his duties. Tofana's famous specific was obtained by Mg original device which maintained its popularity all through the dark days of Italian intrigue. Aqua Tofana was procured from the foam from the lips of a pig, first poisoned with prussic acid and scourged to a frenzy while in the death. throes. The effects of this poison were very sudden and secret, and the chances of discovery much less than by giving the crude drug. The Marquise de Brinv-illiers, whose countless abominations will hardly brook re-numeration, brings us nearer to modern times; she evinced a medical knowledge horribly complete and methodical in dealing with her victims. ENGLISHWOMEN AND STRANGE POISONS. Mention may be made here of one of very few poisoners iound among the ranks of English women of high birth and gentle breeding. Fhougli the ill-starred Mrs. Turner, whose ex- periments in chemistry cost her her life, paid the penalty for the crime E he was only accessory to, there is little dopbt that the beautiful and evil Countess of Somerset, the heroine of two un- savoury divorces and numerous intrigues, was aersonally resnonsible for the death of the man

whose life she had marred by her wickedness while he lay a helpless prisoner in the Tower. It was natural that in medireval days poison preventives were greatly in demand. Agate, swallowed in fragments, was regarded both as a preventive and cure. Opals were much worn in the belief that they turned pale in the pre. sence of poison, and Venetian glass was said to shiver at its, anpnoach. In a rhinoceros horn goblet poison was supposed to effervesce, and pea- coc.l:s would ruffle their feathers and scream at its presence.

TheF imple, monkish teachers of Anglo-Saxon Englund had simple cures and charms: perhaps the poisons of their experience were less potent to deal with.

"Ere yet the danger cometh," says the leech, " bruise ye well the hindmost part of radish with clots, boil in holy water, wherein ye have washed a black snail: if this be supped from the churchyard bell, so may no man harm thee with poison:"

JOURNALIST.

~.,^.P%+o‘^•-%.,•M,e\,/•.N,,..‘e.‘,..-\.^%, CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. Ni-,N,A,...,Ne•'N.,-wNe•a-v.‘e.,\,,y

JOIN MAYNARD, PILOT-HERO.

As we have all been reading and thinking during recent months about heroism at sea I propose to tell to my readers a story which is very familiar to the boys and girls of America but which 'may not be known -in England. The name of John Maynard is still kept in mind amongst the pilots of North America. He was pilot on board a steamer which was going to the town of Buffalo, one summer afternoon. At that time the steamers had. no boats. Smoke came up from under the deck, and the captain sent a sailor down to see what was the matter. The man soon came back in terror, and said, "Captain, the ship is on fire!" Then rose a fearful cry of" Fire! fire! the ship is on fire!" Buckets of water were thrown down, but it was of no use; for they had tar and resin on board, and n3thing could save the ship. The passengers rushed about to find the pilot, and asked, "How far are we from Buffalo?"

"Seven miles," was the reply. "How long before we can reach it ? " "In three quarters of an hour, if the steam keeps up." "Is there any hope of our lives?" "Go to the fore part of the ship," said the pilot, "and I will save you if I can." Men, women and children were soon crowded in the fore part of the ship, and John Maynard, their brave pilot, was left by himself at the helm. Clouds of smoke rose up, and the flames burst through them. The captain called out through his trumpet, "John Maynard !" "Ay, ay, sir." "Are you at the helm ?" " Ay, ay, sir." "Head her south-east, and run her on shore."

Near and more near they came to the shore. Again the Captain cried out, "John Maynard!" The voice in reply was very feeble, "Ay ay, sir." - • •

Can you bold on five minutes more, John ?" "God help me I will." _ , . .

The fire was round him fiercely blazing, when the ship was sent right on shore. Every man, woman' and child was saved when John Maynard died at his awful post of duty.

THE COMPANY WHO TRY. Yes, I love the litt'e winner, With the medal and the mark ; He has gained the prize he sought for, He is joyous as a Jar k. Every one will baste to praise him, He is on the honor list ; I've a tender thought, my darlings, r the one who tried and missed. One ? Alt me ! They count by thousands, Those who have not gained the race, . .

Though they did their best and fairest, Striving for the winner's pace. Only few can reach the laurel, Many see their chance flit by ; I've a tender thought, my darlings, For the earnest baud who try. 'Tis the trying that is noble, If you're made of sterner stuff Than the laggards who are daunted When the bit of road is rough. All will prize the happy winners, nut when they have hurried by, I've a song to cheer, my darlings, The great company who try. CAUGHT IN HIS OWN TRAP. A TRUE STORY.

A friend of Dean Swift one day sent him a turbot as a present, by a servant-lad, who had frequently. been on similar errands, but had never received anything from the dean. He opened the study door, and putting the fish on the floor, cried out rudely : "My master has sent you a turbot." " Young man," said the dean," is that the way you deliver a message? Let me teach you better manners. Sit down in my chair. We will change places, and I will show you how to behave in future. The dean went out, then came to the door, and making a low bow, said: "Sir, my _ciaster sends you his kind com- pliments, and hopes you are well, and requests your acceptance of a small -present." " Indeed !" replied the boy: "Return him my best thanks; and there is half-a-crown for yourself." The dean, thus caught in his own trap, laughed heartily, and gave the boy a crown for his ready wit. TROUBLESOME ENGLISH.

The Liverpool tramway authorities have been trying to make an announcement to passengers. Some time ago this notice was posted in the Liverpool cars: " Passengers are requested to pay no more pennies than the conductor in their presence punches holes in their tickets for." This was criticised, and another effort was made and posted:: "Passengers are requested to pay no more pigmies than for which the conductor in their presence punches holes in their tickets." This too was pronounced a failure, and the officials concluded that language had not yet been invented which would express what they felt. And yet we know exactly what the official notice meant. Now I wonder if any of my readers could give a clear and simple state- ment of the request. If so perhaps the Liverpool Corporation would be grateful.
