WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY.

ITS 250th ANNIVERSPLRY.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
The /Act of Uni

Warrington Nonconformity had a notable birthday. It came into being upon the memorable Sunday of August 1662, when two thousand of the clergy of the Church of England yielded homes and livings rather than give assent to the Act of Uniformity. In this great cleavage Nonconformity had its beginning and among its bravest sufferers was Robert Yates, the Rector of Warrington. The birthday of Nonconformity is remem- bered with mingled feelings. We sorrow at the blindness that ejected and persecuted brave and saintly men ; with equal sincerity we honour those who faced the loss of all things rather than be false to conscience,and in this homage every true English heart will join. Conscience governed the brave Two Thousand. They had no other motive, no other spring of action. In a thousand corners of the kingdom grim and deadly warfare was waged, but it was a noiseless strife. Passive suffering was their armour and save that they taught and wept they raised no weapon against their persecutors. That Warrington was a home of early Nonconformity is a fact of deepest interest and it will be a congenial task to tell of its Rector and the worthy share he bore in the toil and suffering of bitter and memorable days._ _ _

Of Dissent as the forerunner of Noncon- formity it will not be necessary here to write.

THE OLD PA:

From this church Robert Yates was eject in the foreground of our picture, was ;built altered owing to the raising of the roof.

There were those who had separated them- selves from the Established Church in many places and from many causes, and the neighbourhood of Warrington, though per- haps not the town itself, had both Quakers and Dissenters. It will only be necessary for the purpose of our present task to recall the steps which led to the ejection of the clergy in 1662.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
'ormity of 1662.

lose their livings, and become outcasts, or they must be false to their most cherished beliefs.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE ACT WAS.

The Act insisted upon three things. That every minister should publicly profess his "unfeigned assent and consent" to every- thing that is in the Book of Common Prayer, that he should renounce the Solemn League and Covenant, and pledge himself to passive obedience.

This last pledge involved a profession of belief in the divine right of Kings,and though many in that day could make it with sincerity it was impossible to the men who had sent King Charles I. to the block. Equally impossible was the first demand, for the Prayer Book was compiled to meet opposing vidws and contained, as it does to this day, much that was contradictory. The penalties imposed under the Act pro- claimed its object as plainly as did its pro- visions. The minister who failed before St. Bartholomew's Day August 24th, 1662, to publicly announce his assent would be ejected from his living. There was peculiar bitterness, too, in its operation. The day of St. Bartholomew was the anniversary of the day when Protestantism was exterminated in France at the point of the sword. It was moreover the period when the tithes were falling due,

,ISH CHURCH.

- - - ~_ .;. -

;ted in August, 1662. The chancel, which is tin 1353 and is still standing, though slightly

and as the clergy then depended largely upon their tithes they must leave the year's income to be received by those who would follow them.

The three months which lay between May and August left little time for conference. Travelling and postal facilities were not numerous or rapid. Of united action there appears to have bren little. Each man decided for himself. Four hundred and fifty had given up their livings before St. Bartholomew came, and on the August Sun- days of 1662, after tearful farewells had been spoken, fully eighteen hundred ministers walked from their pulpits to face whatever an unpromising future might bring. In the list are some imperishable names, Richard Baxter had been already driven out. John Owen, John Howe, Philip Henry, John Flavel spoke their last words from a parish pulpit on August 17th. Soo too did Bartholomew Wesley and Samuel Annesley, names familiar to Metho- dists as the grandfathers of John Wesley.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RESTORATION.

Under Oliver Cromwell England enjoyed an almost full measure of religious freedonfin afamous declaration it was decreed that"none be compelled to conform to the public religion by penalties or otherwise; but that endeav- ours be used to win them by sound doctrine, and the example of a good conversation." Whether Puritan or Episcopalian in sympathy, or whether Presbyterian or Con- gregational was not allowed to matter. Crom- well believed in a Broad Church and would have all the ministers maintained by the State. Such an atmosphere was favourable to Puritanism and it gave promise of becom- ing the dominating factor in English religion. The failure of Richard Cromwell to main- tain bps father's policy and his quiet with- drawal from office brought England to a crisis. The standing army, a body of trained and stubborn soldiers, was now left without a controlling hand. The outlook was gloomy indeed and gave deep concern. There seemed nothing for it but to fall back on tradition and fill the throne, and to this, though with many misgivings, the most devoted supporters of Cromwell were forced to agree.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
PURITANS AND ROYALISTS.

Charles came to England as Charles 11. on Ma 29th,1660, and began the most shameful reign of which English history has record. The Puritans' leaders had taken the precau- tion of extracting from him a promise of religious liberty and he had given his word at Breda, that he would "allow for tender consciences" and that no one should suffer for his religion. But religion and conscience were terms of which Charles hardly knew the meaning. He came to the throne in a whirl of mad and riotous pleasure that debauched England to its farthest corners. The situation would have taxed the skill of the most resourceful ruler, and demanded, especially on its religious side, wide know- ledge and patience. The king's advisers, unfortunately, had decided that it would be an easy task to break and subdue the Quakers and Presbyterians and be avenged for the past. The struggle quickly began. The Bishops were reinstated, and it soon became evident that only one type of minister was to be tolerated and only one kind of religionallowed to flourish. The Church of England, under the shaping hand of Elizabeth, had been designed to contain Puritan and Episcopalian alike. The question had now to be seriously asked whether the ideal of a broad and National Church was to be kept, or whether the Church was to be Episcopalian pure and simple.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ACT OF

UNIFORMITY.

Anxious Puritans had not long to wait for an answer. Charles had returned in 1660. The Convention Parliament of a year later, which Pepys describes as "composed of pro- fane swearing fellows," was fanatical to the point of madness. Hatred of Puritanism blazed out. The old bitterness revived. The Savoy Conference was an attempt at com- promise but came to nothing, and on May 19th, 1662, nearly. two years after his return, Charles 11 gave. his sanction to the Act of Parliament which was to divide the Church of England with a fatal and still-unhealed cleavage. The wording of the Act of Uniformity left no doubt as to its object. It was drastic and ie was bitter. Sheldon, who was responsible more than any other man for its harsh pro- visions, said in blunt and cruel words that it was meant" to compel the Presbyterians to be Non-Conformists or knaves." That is to say its framers knew quite Fell that the Presbyterians and independents could not agree to it without being false to conscience. They knew too much of the character of the men to suppose that they would ever aquiesce. They must either leave the Church,

===== 0002601_19120831_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL NONCONFORMISTS.

Some of the best Nonconformists had their homes in Lancashire and Cheshire, and the neighbourhood of Warrington contributed a few to the number. From Burton wood was ejected Samuel Mather, M.A., eldest son of Richard Mather, the Pilgrim Father; from Whitley, John Machin. The Uaton family of Cheshire (who deserve and who still await a biographer) contributed four if not five to the ranks of the two thousand. Robert Eaton, ejected from Walton, Samuel Eaton silenced at Duckinfield and William Eaton of Bridport. Samuel was the son of Richard Eaton, vicar of Great Budworth. Another son Theophilus, a sturdy puritan had already fled the country through persecution and became the renowned Governor of New Haven. BLACK BARTHOLOMEW'S DAY. Silenced and homeless they faced the dreary days as best they could. To many it meant destitution, to some, after the anguish of heart-break, came the touch of the angel of death. It was Black Bartholomew's Day indeed for the sufferers. It was a black day indeed in the annals of the Church that thrust them out. Bishop Gore has put his reading of history in a piercing sentence: "Nonconfor- mity is the standing witness to the sin and blindness of the Established Church." It does not fall to us to defend the Non- conformists. Nor indeed have they ever needed an apologist. Dr. Horton has written in stately yet chosen words. "It is the sober confession of history that those Two Thousand were not only the best clergyman 1 in the English Church of 1662, but on the whole the noblest, sincerest, the most self- sacrificing group of ministers that ever existed in the Church at one time." They did not leave the Church, they were expelled from it. They were guilty of no fault nor indolence. An Act of Parliament was passed to make them either "knaves or Non-Con- formists." At the cost of homes and livings they acted the part of honest men. Let us now make the best acquaintance we can with the man who as Rector of Warrington was called to make the great choice under the Act of Uniformity. A.M.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====

LADY FLIES ACROSS THE CHANNEL. Another trip by aeroplane from England to France was made on Wednesday by Miss Trehawke Daviea,who was carried as a passenger by Mr. H. J. Astley. Miss Davies has already shown extreme coolness and pluck in her aerial journeys with Mr. Hamel. With his passenger, Mr. Astley left Hendon on Wednesday morn- ing before 8 o'clock. They arrived safely on French soil at Hardelot, near Boulogne, at about 9.30, and con- tinued their journey towards lsay-les-Moulineaux,which was safely reached.

SLAYING SONG OF KUSAWA AFA*.

11 sat by the fig-tree, Inkoos, in the sunlight, • And talked of the crops with my father, Makumbo, And up to the fig-tree came slowly a stranger And sat at some distance, and clapped, and gave greeting, And said, "I would speak with Makumbo Hashumba." A tall man and thin, with a face full of cunning And covered with pock-marks. His youth was still on him, A dandy was he, with his knife ivory-handled— His teeth filed to points —with his comb and his wash-stick, And over his loins was cloth white and sweeping, Not goat-skin, like us who are sons of Matshanga. My father gave greeting, and bade him come nearer. And offered him snuff. For long while we were silent 'limn rapid and harshly - the way of his people— The stranger gave tongue with strange words and much gesture : "0 Chief, I am Kwatza, the son of Makaia, Makaia is chief on the low veld, the Pungwi Flows over our lands when the rains are in season— Gorongoza are we, of the land of Barui. CHILDREN ACT WORKING WELL.

Free Church Council on Ignorance of Its

Provisions.

Some time ago the temperance committee of the National Free Church Council decided to inaugurate an inquiry into the working of the Children Act throughout the country. The whole of England and Wales was covered, and the replies constitute a complete survey of the position in England and Wales. Opinion seems to be general that the Act is working fairly well, but that it might be made much more effective if its provisions wete more generally known. " Lack of knowledge of the possibilities and scope of the Act is much too general,' says one secretary. Where religious and philanthropic societies have mastered the Act, and have co-operated with local authorities, it is stated to have proved most beneficial. 'I he inquiry shows that the number of licences is being steadily reduced, but in the words of the Notthatnptonshire Federation secretary, "there is a general feeling that existing laws ara too slow in action."

In regard to the adoption cf the temperance syllabus in the schools, it is stated that there is much room for improvement.

"KISSING THE BOOK."

At an inquest at Farnworth on Saturday, Coroner Brighouse commented strongly on " the stupidity of witnesses with regard to the new form of taking the oath." To a witness, who was about to go through the old formula, be said, "Please do not kiss the book; you will get a microbe. (Laughter.) Parliament has passed an Act doing away uith that procedure, because they think there are shoals of microbes, ,Xc., walking about the Testament, and you will be liable to get all sorts of diseases and complaints if you kiss it. Now that Parliament has been so generous and does not want people to do it, everybody is stupid enough to hanker after doing it." (Laughter).

The Examiner, Saturday, August 31, 1912

darkness. For sleep, he has 5 hours of dark- ness and 3 hours of light, which light is not only wasted to him, but sometimes prevents sleep.

The second diagram shows that the same man would have about 4 hours of sunlight and one hour of darkness in his leisure, with the additional advantage of an extra hour of darkness during sleep. 8. The gain of one hour of sunlight each evening, yields more advantages than would a half holiday each week. For the whole period in each year covered by the Bill, the gain will amount to 154 hours sunlight, the equivalent to say 16 Bank Holidays. 9. The cost of artificial lighting will be reduced, as it will not be necessary to light up until an hour later than at present. The total money saving to the nation will be not less than £2,500,000 a year. 10. The whole cost of the scheme is simply the moving of the hands of the clock forward one hour in April and backward one hour in September. 11. The enjoyment of life will be increased and the health of the people improved. 12. Daylight Saving Bills have been con- sidered by the Parliaments of Canada, Victoria (Australia), and New Zealand. In each case a Select Committee has been appointed, has received evidence, and has reported strongly in favour of the Bill. In Great Britain and Ireland, 516 City Corpora- tions and Town Councils, as well as.many leading Societies, Trades' Unions, Clubs, Chambers of Commerce, etc., have passed resolutions in favour of the Bill. 13. More than 280 members of the House of Commons in tirt Parliament of 1911 have expressed their goodwill towards the Bill. 14. ALL IN A GARDEN FAIR.

BY GEORGE A. WADE. Author of "The Secret of Life," "Eighty M. es an Hour," "A Narrow Escape," "The RC*" of Towton," "Lady Aluriers Lovers," tr.

From the further end of the lovely old garden came the sweet voice of the singer. It ivas a man's voice, and he was evidently no mean tenor who was singing. The country maiden who came strolling through the garden gate, with her hands full of ox-eye daisies, turned smilingly to listen as the words floated to her from the far distance:

"Oh, winds that traverse the ocean, Oh, swallows that skim the air, To my loved one say I wait her i All n a garden fair." The girl looked round her, with pleasure beaming in her dark eyes. Yes, the garden was certainly very fair; sweet, indeed, was its appearance on this early morning of warm July. Its centre was a large close-trimmed lawn, soft as velvet to the feet, with two mag- nificent apple trees growing one at each end of it; the sides of the garden consisted of wide borders wherein grew all the beautiful flowers that we know so well for their rich colours, their wondrous fragrance, -and their graceful forms. There were scores of Canter- bury bells—white, pink, and blue; there were sweet-smelling stocks and mignonette in pro- fusion ; there were roses—red, white, yellow, and pink; there were glowing geraniums and flaunting yellow calceolarias ; there were lilies, asters, and campanulas, as well as other

flowers in plenty. And, at the far end of the garden, as the girl saw it, there was the high rock-bank, with its stonecrop a golden yellow, and almost covering it; with its foxgloves bowing proudly; with its graceful ferns, and its blue lobelia in clumps; a rock-bank rising so high as to shut out from view, or nearly so, the lawn behind it that went right up to the old manor house, now a modernised dwelling. J On this lawn sat the man who was singing. His profession of artist was clear from the easel that stood before him as he Sat there painting. His subject was the very girl now standing at the gate, only he was painting her as she had appeared a day or two before in the field of corn behind the house. And he fang as he painted, for his heart was gay. Caroline stood and listened to him. l'hen ,here came into her face a troubled look, and :here passed through her mind a question that was almost unnecessary, for she felt sure she knew its answer. Who was the "loved one," to whom was to come the message of the winds and the swallows? Caroline had little doubt that she knew. Only the night before had not this artist- gentleman, this summer visitor to her mother's house in the lovely Dorset country round Wimborne, stood with her in this sams garden and whispered how much he bed grown to love her? Had he not asked her to come to London, where he would chew her at the splendid things that her heart and eyes had so longed to see for years? Had he not promised to love her truly, to marry her, and to make her a fine lady? It was a most tempting offer to the country girl whose life up to this had been far from easy or abounding. in pleasure. For Mrs. Shelston, her mother, was a widow, and had to work hard to keep herself and Caroline in a fair way of life. The mother did fine lace- work for many ladies who had known her for years, and she had a good clienthle, it is true. But the work was not highly paid, and the toil was constant if decent wages had to be made from it. As for Caroline, she had learnt shorthand and typing at the technical school in Wimborne, and she now received twelve shillings a week from a firm near the Minster there, to whom she acted as typist and secretary. But though twelve shillings a week as typist might not be bad pay for a girl in a small country town—a girl just about nine- teen years of age, who had no small preten- sions to beauty with her tall, willowy form, her dark eyes and hair, her clear skin, and her gentle manners—yet, when Caroline read of the pretty women in society, and of their pleasant life in London, she was, like many others in her own rank and station, greatly led to envy them, and to wish she were one of them. Al! such desires and wishes, however, had seemed about as far from realisation as pos- sible until this Mr. Norman Taylor had come on a chance visit to the district round 'Vim- borne, borne and had been recommended by a friend to stay at Mrs. Shelston's cottage as being a handy place for getting about. Then gradually the sly glances and the looks in the artist's eyes had told Caroline only too well that he admired her greatly. By degrees' as the two got more intimate, the artist had spoken to her of other things than painting, and only the night before lie had confessed that he loved her, and had offered her the coveted pleasures already mentioned if she would go to London with him. Now, Caroline ought never to have listened to his protestations of love for a minute, and in her own heart she knew it! Had not Frank Stanley and she been supposed for some time to be more or less engaged to each other by all the village? Had they not kept company for months, after knowing one another closely for years? Was it not certain that Frank loved her, and her only, with a passionate and all-devouring affection? And had she not told him more than once that she reciprocated his love? It was true that there was no formal engagement between them, but there was a mutual understanding, and Caroline knew it well. Indeed, until the coming of this artist to stay at her mother's for a month Caroline Shelston never dreamt. for a moment of being anybody's wife but Frank's. Mr. Stanley was only a clerk in a ware- house at Bournemouth, a few miles away. His salary was but thirty shillings weekly, j nd it was very unlikely that Miss Shelston's Ireama of London society, and of figuring therein, would ever be fulfilled if she married this young clerk. But his heart was true as steel to her; he would make an excellent hus- band; they would be very happy together in some cheap country cottage like her mother's, and the girl knew it! let there lay on her soul the glamour of the artist's proposal as she stood at the gate listening to the song he sang. , She knew it well, for she had often heard Frank sing it too, though his voice was not nearly so rich in tone as that of his new rival. She had been in the woods gathering flowers, and she had brought back from their out- skirts a magnificent bunch of big ox-eye daisies. The winds were gently breathing round her tall, willowy figure, and the swift swallows were darting past her to their nests beneath the house-roof, as her dark eyes grew more and more troubled, and her face became more rigid and pale. She thought herself unseen yet by the singer, but she was mistaken, for Norman Taylor had caught sight of her as soon as she had reached the gate, but pretended not to have done so as he went on with his painting and his song. And again there rose' on the air the words of the chorus she had so often joined in with Frank. " To my loved one say I wait her All in a garden fair."

"By Jove! Miss Shelston—Carrie—how beautiful you look this afternoon," said the artist, at length seeming to observe her from the lawn behind the bank, and coming down the path towards her. His face glowed and his eyes shone, whilst the girl blushed deeply at his compliments. "What a sensation you would cause in the Row! " he whispered, insinuatingly, as he stood by her. "You could give all the ladies there long points and beat them!" It was just this sort of flattery that touched Caroline's weak spot, as the man had quickly discovered. They !int: talking for some minutes on the seat inside the lovely old gar- den, and when they moved it was with a smile of triumph that he accompanied her to the house. And the charming white rose—" Mar- garet Dickson "—that she wore had been plucked and given her by him.

"It's no use, I can't stand this deadly dull life any longer! " the girl exclaimed, about a month after the artist had left Wimborne. " I was made for something better, as he so often said. Frank's all right for the average girl, I suppose, and I know what a good fel- low. lie is. But I could not possibly settle

down to prosaic love and life like that. I was born for high society, as Norman said more than once. And I really think he was right, too. At any rate,. I cannot remain about Vilmborne all the rest of my days." She sat in the garden, and she was alone, for Mrs. Sheleton had gone to tea with a friend on this Saturday afternoon late in August, and Frank had not yet arrived from Bournemouth. She drew from her pocket a letter that had come that morning, about which she had not yet told her mother. It was from Norman Taylor, asking her to join him in London, when he would marry her and \ place her in society, where he would do her- self justice. If she found the proposal agree- able he named a station where he would meet her on the journey to town. She bad been thinking so much of the letter ell the day thus far, that her typing at the office had been very indifferently done, and her employer had imagined she was not very well, and had told her to hurry off home an hour before the proper time. She was allured by the proposal in the letter, and her mind was now practically made up to this desperate step before her. As she sat musing in that old garden by herself she seemed again to hear the artist singing the song. And in the mind of the Dorset girl there came the recol- lection of all that this garden meant to her, like a whiff of sweetness amidst much that was leading her astray. "Yee, I shall miss you," she said, "you lovely garden and flowers! Just as I shall miss ' dear old Frank, too! He will sing here as before, I do not doubt, when be finds another woman to love him better than I have ever done. But I can't stay here; I really can't! I must have more life, a -wider sphere, or I shall be throwing away all my talents. Ho said so; and he Is right. Besides, doesn't he love me and want to marry me "

DAYLIGHT SAYING.

A SHORT EXPLANATION OF

THE BILL.

1. The Bill proposes to utilize during the I summer months some of the early morning sunlight, which is now often wasted while we sleep, and thereby to add an hour's sun- light at the end of the day. 2. This is to be accomplished by moving the hands of the clock forward one hour, at 2 a.m. on the third Sunday in April, and backward one hour, the third Sunday in September. 3. This will entail : (I) No carlier rising than at present, because we shall continue to rite at the same hour of the clock. (2) No alteration in Railway Time Tables, excepting with regard to Continental Traffic, a very small propoition of the whole. (3) No loss of sleep. 4. The chief advantage to be gained is that we should have daylight for an hour later every evening during the Summer months. This would be most valuable in May, June, July, August and September. It would be specially appreciated by those who take their holidays in the latter months, as their enjoyment would not be curtailed by darkness setting in so soon after tea. •5. The effect of the scheme would be to make the day fit man's requirements more closely. We should simply replace a work- ing day containing a certain number of hours of sunlight, by another containing one hour of sunlight more, and that in the only period now available, to most people, for leisure and recreation. _

6. The following diagrams illustrate how this will operate.

Concerning Red.—ln shopland, there is just now,quite an epidemic of rid, a state of affairs to be deplored, by the majority of girls and women. For it cannot be denied that however picturesque red in all sh ides—from vivid primitive hue, to the more subtle ce is, s—may be in a crowd. or at a distance ; at close quarters it is found to :how up the least defect in a complexion. To the blow e with a fresh colour, bright red spells a lack of refinement, tit Ve in proximity to pallor, it umally succeeds in hinging out any suspicion of a yePow tint Woman a ith drab hair should rever wear reds, while to those possessed of sallow complexions, dark wine hues are particularly unbecoming. As a matter if fact, red belongs absolutely to the iymnette with a sufficiency of colour to look well. Old-fashioned red costumes, trimmed with aniline, have appeared, and have quite an old-world Christmas card effect. Oriental Rugs.—People are often heard to complain when, hiving purchased an Oriental rug, it comes home and is laid down, that it has not the same depth of colouring as when seen in the shop. This state of affairs is generally due to the position of the rug. When shown in the shop, care is always taken to have the pile towards the light, 10081190 that helps to bring out the beauty and bloom of its colouring. If purchasers always exercised the same care, there would be fewer disappointments in this direction. To clean Oriental rugs, the best and most successful procedure is to wash them—a square foot at a time—with a soft brush and olive oil soap. The soap must then be rinsed off, first with clear warm water and then clear cold water Lastly the portion cleansd must be well rubbed with soft cloths till dry.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Woman's Suffrage in the United States.—The Presi- dential election in the United States, which takes place in November, will be the fl:st in which women voters will take any, considerable part, and Mr. Roosevelt's support of Womans Suffrage, marks a further stage in the American progress of the cause. Hitherto Colonel licosevelt has been generally regarded as an anti- feminist, but he now declass in favour of equal citizen rigt ts for both sexea, on the score that working a omen need the vote as a weapon with which to defend their economic interests. In six States, women already possess the "full suffrage," and the addition of Wash- ington and California to the four original suffrage states—Utah, Idaho, Wyoming, and Colorado—meant that fully a million women will be entitled to vote in the Presidential election. In some of the States, women have no vote of tny kind, but in most of them they have been granted the "school suffrage." In the six States referred to, wornee have the vote on the adult suffrage basis encl it includes the municipal vote, the vote for the State Legislature and the vote for the President, for which purpose the voters elect delegates who then elect the President. Of the other candidates +Abe are its the running for the Presidency, Mr. Wood- row Wilson has not declared himself a suffragist, nor has Mr. Mir, and it leeks as if Mr. Roosevelt will have the solid support of the feminist movement in the States. Hunger Strikes.—The forcible feeding of the two ornen who were sentenced to penal servitude, for attempting to set fire to a Dublin theatre, as again revived discussion as to the treatment of the militant suffragists. Whatever views we may hold with regard to women's suffrage, we cannot help feeling shocked at, such repulsive methods. And yet it is difficult to suggest any other way of dealing with it determined " hunger strike." In their own interests these mis- guided women cannot be permitted to commit suicide by a slow and painful procets. Nor on the other hand, can the authorities be expected to accede to the demand for preferential treatment. It is our boast that there is one law for all in this country, and if a person commits incendiary acts, he or she must suffer the penalty, irrespective ef the motives which prompted their commitment. "Without Encumbrance."—The use of the word "encumbrance" as a synon3m for children, cannot le too strongly condemned, and the system associated with it, is equally blameworthy. These words "without encumbrance " may he found in hundreds of advertise- ments for garden( ra, housekeepers, coachmen, chauffeurs, stewards, butlers, &c., as well as for appoint- ments in public iestitutions. A great deal is heard nowadays about our declining birth rate, and the evils that are expected to result from It, but is it any wonder that the hirthrrite should decrease when a ban is put upon children, when, as so often happens, public appointments are closed to the heads of families and when their chances of promotion are jeoperdited? It is easy to understand that in certain situations, such as master and matron of a workhouse, a large family of small children would be very undesirable, but in rune' cases, better accommodation might he Drovided for little ones, or array g4ments made for officers to live out. In any case, the hateful word " encumbrance" as applied to children ought to disappear from public notices. It is an insult to our sociA life and a disgrace to any public body that uses it. Ex-Sultan's View on Large Ilate.--Mulai Henn, the ex-Sultan of Morocco, having retired from business, is now in the enjoyment of a pension of .C 15,000 a year fr m the French Government. That is sufficient to enable him to enjoy all the refinements of civilisation, for which purpose be is now on a visit to France, where he has been expressing his opinions of vcomers' fashions. Ile does not approve of the large bats worn by Frenchwomen, and thinks the small ones of the men much more becoming. Possibly his opinion my not t ohltogether without influence in the world of millinery, for the exSultan is being somewhat " lionised " in Paris, and he says that the Fienchwoman's hair and face are much too beautiful to be hidden by large hats, a shiewd complirmit which will no doubt be duly appreciated. Freakish Millinery.—The millinery of the moment may be, and doubtless is, smart and eashing, but it is most certainly freakish. Never were hats less trimmed, some straw hats have just one feather at the back, and that constitutes the sole garniture. Nothing in shapes seems to bizarre, coal scuttles and helmets, four cornered and tricornes abound. One shape, the Robespierre, has soft outlines, and being guiltless of corners is becoming to old and young. A novel idea is the bat of chamois leather in its natural colour with a brim of straw trimmed with quills. The beaver tat will carry all before it this autumn the plush bat following it very closely. Felt h ts have been worn more or less all the summer. Milliners shops are now full of white felts in a variety of shapes, but all small in size. The Coarse Lace Blouse.—No kind of blouse, intro- duced during the last two a oars, is quite as handsome as that made of coarse lace, with a basque. It has been very much worn with lingerie dresses this season, and will doubtless influence the shape of that useful garment the blouse, which as autumn advances is made in heavier rusterials, such as velveteen, coarse ribtei silk, and crepe do chene. The new models of the last named materials have very cheap basques, and are more like small coats than blouses. With Robespierre collars, j %hots, and frills Emile way up the sleeve, as well as round tho wrist, they are certainly quaint, although not suited to any but slim women. The coarse lace blouse is capable of great variety, and this possibly Is one of its many attractions. .1 t can be built of so many different kinds of lace, or of insertion arranged vertically or diagonally, and joined by lace beading. A . net or lace yoke then completes this fascinating garment, which can be easily made by anyone who has time at her disposal, and a little skill in the use of the

needle.

CONGREGATIONAL UNION, --- Membership Increase of Over 12,000.

The autumn meetings of the Congregational Union of England and Wales open in Manchester on October 13th under the chairmanship of Dr. W. F. Adeney, M.A. On Monday, 14h, at 330, the Lord Mayor of Manchester will hold a reception in the Town Hall. At the evening meeting in the Albert Hall, arranged by the Temperance Committee, Dr. Adeuey will pre- side, and the speakers include the Bishop of Lincoln and the Rev. J. Morgan Gibbon. At 5 30 in the afternoon the union sermon will be delivered in the Albert Hall by the Rev. H. C. Carter, MA, of Immanuel Church, Cambt idge. On Tuesday morning the chairman's address will be followed by a paper from l'rincipal W. B. Selbie, D.D , on " The Widespread Indifference to Religion." A gleat demonstration is arranged for the evening in the Free Trade Hall to commemorate the 250th anniveisary of the 1662 ejectment, the speakers being the Rev. C. Silvester Horne, M.P., the Rev. Sidney Berry, and Dr. J. D. McClure. On Wednesday int,rning the Rev. W. Reason, M.A.. presents the tepot t of the Social Service Committee on the Labour unrest, and at night the London Missionary Society holds its usual valedictory meeting in the Free Trade Hall, when leave will be taken of a large number of missionaries. • At the Colonial Missionary Society's meeting on the following evening, Mr. A. Hutton will preside, and among the speakers are Bishop Welldon, Dean of Manchester, and the Rev. Flank Lenwood, MA, The union will meet in good spirits since from figures supplied to us by Mr. John Minshull, of the Finance Depaitment, there is an increase on the year in the membership of the w orld's Congregationalism of no fewer than 12,640 members and 28,825 Sunday school scholars. Last year there was an increase of 7,178 members, but a decreaFe of 14,191 in the roll of Sunday school scholars. There is also an increase in the number of churches, chapels and mission , stations; the total world membership is now 1,479,869.

"TWO MINDS WITH 13UP A SINGLE THOUGHT:, This is the title of a particularly interesting and fascinating book vvrilten by the Zancigs. It gives a remarkably clear and practical explanation of the wonderful performances for which the Zancigs have become famous, and its authors maintain that any two people whose ideas and sympathies have been proved to be slong similar lines can with experience and prac- tice become Equally successful in interpreting from time to time each other's thoughts' ideas and conclu- sions. PE ople do not now-a-days laugh at the idea of communion of thought, nor imagine as In the days of old that the apparently remark ably results are obtained by some unseen trickery ; and all who are interested in the modern development of this science cannot do better than procure this interesting volume.

RECREATION

Many whom she Truly loved, and she cried out in her bitterness of heart. For she recognised now that she really loved Frank. Stanley, not Norman Taylor; that life would be unendurable without him, even in "society." Her thoughts ran back to the beautiful fair garden, to the mother who had done so much for her, to the man who had proved his love by deeds, and that often. Yes, it was Frank she loved,' and she knew it now. What a fool she had been! she toll herself. Was it too late to turn back, to repent, to undo the wrong? The tempter said Yes," but her good angel said No." And that good angel, aided by the prayers of the two at home, prevailed mightily: For, at Basing- stoke, the first stop, the girl got out of the train. .

She sent off a telegram at once to her mother. It sold, "Back to-morrow night. Letter left for you only a joke. Am on a visit here with a friend to-night." And early on the Sunday morning that tele- gram woe delivered to Mrs. Shelton and Frank. The man shouted in his joy as he read it aloud. . _

..„ "What did I tell you, Mrs. Shelston? Didn't I know Carrie through and through? It was but a little joke of hers, you see, after all."

He grasped the widow's hand in his ex- citement, nevertheless; and he saw that her eyes were full of tears of joy, whilst his on certainly felt moist. . For some minutes she could not speak, so great was the tug at her heart-strings. Then she took her handkerchief and wiped her eyes, as she said: "Thank Heaven!" . .

MR. BRAMWELL BOOTH.

Mr. Bramwell Booth, who has been appoin- ted General and Commander-in-Chief of the Salvation Army, was born in Halifax fifty-six years ago. In many respects he has inherited the late General's characteristics. He is tall, grey-haired, and clean-shaven. He has been

the chief of staff since 1880. Thirty years ago he married a lady from the Army ranks, the daughter of Dr. Soper of Plymouth. They have , seven children. For several years she has superintended the Women'a Department.

She sent a telegram to Mr. Taylor, say- ing that she was coming; and she left a short note for her mother, and another for Frank, asking them to forgive her, but leaving no ad- dress for them to write to. She just said that she had gone to London to be married to the artist because she loved him better than any- one else. It was a sad scene that evening in the cot- tage near Wimborne, whero the frantic mother and the stricken young clerk both came to the house 'too late to -do anything at once, for the last train had left for the bier city some time before they arrived at the old home. The man W ftei the first to speak after a long silence, during \which the sobs of the mother, and the half-whispered word "Villain," had been the only breaks. -" She'll come back, Mrs. Shelston," ha "Carrie 'll come back all right. For he doesn't lore her, and he won't marry her. And you need not fear dear Carrie'e doing anything wrong when once her eycs are opened as to his real character. She's quick enough to re,..e that, is Carrie! And you needn't fear your girl's going astray! She's all right in her 'heart; I'll answer for Carrie there."

The mother took his hand tenderly in her own, and her eyes looked the thanks and affection that she could not speak with her lips. Then the man's brave soul spoke again. "Let's say not a word about it to anyone," he whispered. "Let them simply think Carrie has gone ofT for a- day or two. We shall gain time, at any rate, and I feel sure that something will happen to help Us by then." "Oh! why did she leave such a good, lov- ing fellow as you, Frank?" asked the stricken mother. "How kind you have always been to her and to me. And then to treat you like this!"

"Hush!" he said, soothingly. "Don't you Bee? We need only wait for her, not make a stir that will spoil everything. Carrie's heart is of gold, and her sense is excellent. She's off the task just now, no doubt, but she'll ace how things stand right enough, if only we have the good sense to leave het alone for a day or two. I'd trust her against the world." You don't mind kneeling and saying a word of prayer with me for God to protect her?" said Mrs. Shelton, quietly. "Not at all," replied the young man. "In- deed, I was about to suggest it. A thousand times better to ask our Father to watch over her and guide her than to set all tbs.-town by the ears with news that would prove fatal." Then the two knelt down and prayed, and it is certain that both their hearts were lighter when they rose again. "She'll come back, all right, you be sure," raid the man. "She loves this dear old gar- den too much to stay away long from it. And she knows well, too, that I am waiting for her here. But it will not be by day that she will return; it will be the pleasant evening twi- light that will bring our Carrie back to us, Mrs. Shelston.':

Frank Stanley evidently knew the girl better than anyone, ne the sequel proved. She had gone away, tempted by a sudden impulse and allured by glowing words. She had for- saken the true for the false ; had left those who really loved het for one who was an alluring traitor; had set out from the beloved fair garden and the charming peace of Dor- setsbire to the noise and bustle of London. . _

; But Carrie never got to Loridon at all ; never met Norman Taylor at Surbiton, as he had suggested. In fact, the girl had hardly got well off in the express from Southampton, which had been her starting point for the metropolis, before he recognised the folly of what she had undertaken, before she saw the inner meaning that lay under those smooth phrases of the artist's letter. Then. her soul rose against the wrong she was cloths/ to so

anxiety was but just beginning to Ilnd release. "" What did I tell you?' he exclaimed. again, stopping suddenly in his walk, as though he wished to make the matter very plain to the girl's mother, in order to strengthen his own faith. "What did I tell you? And our darling Carrie'll come home again to-night, when the ta Hight has fallen, never again to-leave us, you may be sure! Well, we'll give .her a right hearty welcome, and no mistake! But, mind, never a word to her in future about this, Mrs. Shelston ! It's only been a joke, you see And r e must always let Carrie think that We regarded it as such—eh?" He smiled somewhat pitifully as he said the last words slowly. But then his face brightened once more, for was she not com- ing back pure and good as ever, she, his dar- ling, his Carrie? When evening twilight gathered round there were two who gazed long and eagerly adown the country road that led from Wim- borne to the village. But for a long time hey gazed and strained their eves in vain In the' deepening night. Then the quickened senses of the mother at last sighted what was enough for her, and she broke into a joyful cry. "She's coming, Frank! She's coming! There she is at the turn of the road ! "

The returning wanderer drew nearer and nearer the garden-gate. I3ut her steps were slow, and she hesitated when, she saw ro mother or lover there to welcome her. How would they receive her? she asked herself. Had her fault been already irreparable? She almost stood still, so heavy had her feet seemed to become this last two hundred yards, so sad her heart, so surging her blood. Then suddenly, through the darkness of the night, from the near-end of the garden she knew so well, there came the voice of a singer. Oh! how her heart leaped for joy as she recognised the voice and the song! It came like the music of an angel floating to ner as she placed her hand on the gate.

" Oh, windi that traverse the ocean, Oh, swallows that skim the air, To my loved one say I wait her All in a tzarden fair."

There burst on the air a joyful cry from the lips of the girl. At that moment two figures hurried to the gate, which stood open for her.

"Frank! Frank!" came from the faint- ing C%rrie, as she stumbled forward into the old garden. "MY darling!" he murmured, $e he caught' her in his arms and kissed her cheeks pmstanately. Orford.

--- - CHOIR AND SUNDAY SCHOOL SERINIONS.—The atonal choir and Sunday school sermons were preached in the church of St. Margaret and All Hallows on Sunday. The preacher in the morning was the Vicar (the her. G. Vale Owen), his sermon having reference to Sunday schools and their work. A children's flower service was held at thr. e o'clock in the afternoon. A large number of children attended and brought plants and bunches of flowers, which they carried up to the altar rail, where they were received by the Vicar. 'Mr. H. J. Westbrook, of Stockton Heath, delivered a very interesting address to the childr, n, taking for his text St. Matthew, vi., 29, " Even Solomon in all his glory, was not arrayed like one a these." The flowers weie afterwards sent to the Warrington Infirmary. The preacher at the 6.30 service was the Vicar. He paid great tribute to the choir, and also the wardens and araescaen, who he said were included in the clergy.

Lymm. LOCAL STUDENT'S SUCCESS.—It is interesting to note that Mr. Wm. Diamond of Lynun has obtained the first prize in " Steam and Heat Engines "as the result of recent examinations, held at the Salford Royal Technical institute. THE LATE MR. ARMSTRONG.—Much interest was created in Booth's Hill on Monday last, when the funeral procession of the late Mr. Jblin Armstrong of Altrincbam, passed through en route for Warrington, where the interment was to take place. The cortege consisted of hearse, two cabs filled with wreaths, and a large number of cabs containing mourners. The drawn blinds along the route denoted the respect with which the deceased gentleman was regarded.

FLOODS AT LYMII.—In L'OMMOD with many other parts of the country the lower portions of the Lymm district have been flooded. Especially E o has this been the case around Heatley where many fields have been under water. In one of Mr. George Allen's fields there has been the unusual sight of clumps of hay resting on the top of a partly submerged hedge. On Saturday last the road on the Warburton side of the bridge by the Heatley Flour Mills was covered over with water to a considerable depth and a farmer had quite a busy time carting people across. file waterfall under the road by Lymm Church has also been much in evidence. ANNIVERSARY AT CHERRY LANE.—The anniversary services of the Cherry-lane Icdependent Methodist Chapel were held on Sunday. Mr It Lewis, of Ashton- in-Makerfleld, was the preacher for the day. In the evening he spoke from the words " Verily I say unto you, except ye be converted, and become as little children, ye shall not enter into the Kingdom of Heaven," and impressed the large congregation presett with his effort. Idr Harold Mercer presided at the organ. Collections on behalf of the chapel funds were taken.

WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL TRIP —On Wednesday about sixty members of the Wesleyan Sunday School and Choir went for a trip to Rhyl. On the outward journey the party had saloon accommodation attached to a last train end so the distance was covered in a quick and pleasant manner. On arrival at the popular North Wales resort Mr Finch kindly provided the com- pany with light refreshments. The floe morning was spent in a happy way and all were ready to participate in the splendid dinner that had been provided for them at the Grosvenor Temperance Hotel. Despite the unwelcome showers that made their appearance in the afternoon the party enjoyed themselves by boating on the Marine Lake, riding on the toy railway, the water chute, and bathing. Lymm was safely reached shortly before 10 p.m. ANOTHER CANAL TRAGEDY.—The Bridgewater Canal has again been the scene of another drowning fatality, this being the second case within two months in the Lymm district alone. The unfortunate victim has again been a child, this time a girl of six years of age named Gladys Lea, youngest aauglater of Mr and Mrs John Lea, farmers, of Dog Bridge, Oughtrington. After tea on Tuesday evening Gladys was with a friend, Edna Clare, and about seven o'cicck they were playing with SOMe twigs on the canal bank at the back of Oughtring- ton schocls when Glady overbalanced and fell into the water. Her companion ran off at once to some houses near by and gave the alarm. In a very short time a number of persons were on the spot where the accident had occurred but the child had disappeared. Dragging operations were commenced but it was not until twu hours later that the body was recovered. Much sympathy for her parents in their sad bereavement has been expressed. The calamity is rendered more tragic by the fact that Mr. Lea had been mowing in the field opposite only a few minutes before the deplorable event occurred.—Mr. T. Ridgway pre- sided over the inquest held on Wednesday evening in the Oughtrington Schools when the verdict of "Accidental Drowning" was given. The funeral took place at Oughtrington Church on Friday afternooon. LYILM URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL . _.• •

EXTRA REPRESENTATIVE FOR STATHAM. The Chairman announced that a petition had been received from the ratepayers of Statham ward request- ing that an extra representative on the C,uncirl enould be granted them. The Clerk submitted statistics which he bad prepared showing the approximate rateable value and number of houses in each vtard. The ngures showed that in 1891 the number of houses in Booths Hill ward was 340 and the rateable value was ,£7,350. Since then 29 new houses had been built at an estimated assessable value of A 795 making the total approximate value £8,145. In Heatley ward in 1894 there were 311 houses with a rate- able value of £8,300. There were 69 additional houses bringing up the approximate rateable value to £10,436 including the salt works. In Lymm ward there were 212 houses in 1894 with assessable value of £8,450. Additional houses built, 30, bringing up approximate assessable value to £9,327. In Statham ward there were 133 houses in 1891 with rateable value of £4,200. Additional houses built, 31, bringing the total approximate rateable value including the Council offices to £5,219. The Clerk explaiued that if the Council considered there was a case Our the granting of an extra represen- tative the procedure would be to make application to the County Council who would cause an inquiry to he made. The question was, could the Council make out a case cn those figures. It was decided that the Clerk write to the Clerk of County Council supplying him with flew es and inquiring what likelihood there'would be of an extra representa- tive being allowed on tha basis of the figures submitted.

ASSAULT ON POLICEMAN.

At Widnes Police Court on Monday, before Alderman Owens and Smith and Major Wariog, a well-known local character, Imo ed MiCunnel Brodigan, of Lugsdale- road, was charged with having teen drunk and dis- orderly, and also with having assaulted P.C. Ford. The police officer, whose right hand was swathed in bandages, stated that he and P.C. Rochford arrested the man on the first charge late on Sunday night. Brodigan refused to go quietly to the station, and his conduct became very violent. He was most abusive, and in filthy language threatened to "drive his clogs through hi.n." lie then threw himself on the ground and hellaved like a n adman, kicking witness in the stomach several times and badly injuring his hands. They had great cifficulty in getting him to the station. Witness atterwards had to be medically treated. P.C. Rochford corroborated. Prisoner denied the second charge. He alleged the. officers acted most brutally towards him because he had failed to pay an old fine. He was quiet, but their cruel grip forced him to throw himself on toe ground.

The minutes were confirmed. , CLIFTON SEWERAGE AND WATERWORKS SCHEME.

The Clifton Sewerage and Waterworks Committee reported that the engineer bad attended a meeting and reported upon the terms upon which the London and North-Western Railway Company were prepared to grant a water supply rising on their railway for the supply of water to the parish. They recommended in so far as the sewage was concerned that the surveyor be instructed to take levels to see whether the existing sewer and surface water drain could not be carried to such a point as to prevent any further nuiance, with a catch pit at the end, to catch any solid matter without having the drain flowing into the Weaver as at present. So far as the water question is concerned they con- sidered the terms laid before them were such as could not be entertained by the parish at present and they recommend the matter lie in abeyance. The recommendations were adopted.

PETTY SESSIONS.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday before Messrs Ockleston and Posnett. Two YOUNG BLOODS.—Alfred Young, Granville- street, and Charles Wood, Brunswick-street, were sum- moned for fighting on the 10th August.—Young : He hit me first. I was on the floor when the constable came.—The Clerk : But were yoa engaged in fighting? Defendant : I must have been. (Laughter).—P.C. Wareing said the men were fighting in Church-street. Wheni he separated them Young said that Wood had commenced the bother by hitting him. Wood alleged that Young was the aggressor, and had kicked him.— Young : He put his head against mine and I put mine a bit nearer and he struck me. (Laughter.) The Chairman: Are you friends again now ? I hope you shook hands when you'd finished, like the boxing men. Defendants said they were friends now. (Laughter.) The Chairman : "Well, if you will shake hands the case will Le dismissed. I like to know an Englishman can fight. (Laughter.)—Defendants under- took to shake hands outside the Court. OLD WOMAN'S ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.—Sarah Moulton, Cooper-street, was charged on remand with having I attempted to commit suicide by drowning herself in the Manchester Ship Canal on Sunday, 18th A-tigust. The Clerk : Do you feel better now ? Defendant : You don't get much better in jail. The Clerk : Will you promise not to do it again? Defendant : Yes. Mr Posnett commented upon the fact that there was no one in Court to look alter the woman. The Clerk suggested toldefendant that she should see the relieving , officer, who would probably do something to assist her.

ST. HELENS.

SUICIDE ON SABBATH MORN.

"Suicide while insane" was the verdict returned at the inquest at St. Helens on Tuesday, on Elizabeth Taylor, a married woman, of 35, Recreation-street, who was drowned on Sunday morning in the St Helens and Sankey Canal. The husband stated that occasionally she took took much drink. His wife was depressed and low-spirited when she had drinking bouts. They bad not had any quarrel or trouble% John Eden, chemical labourer, stated that as he went along the canal side, he saw the woman sitting on a low wall. She appeared depressed and he turned round several times 1,4 see that she was all right, and then went into the works. He had only been there a short time when a man told him that the woman had jumped into the water. Witness succeeded in getting the body to the towing path, and artificial respiration was resorted to withcut avail. GOOD TEMPLARS' DESIRE.

THE HUMAN STOMACH.

How Warmth and Strength and Life Depend Upon IL Ancient Indian Remedy Which Works Marvels in Stomach Trouble.

A healthy man scarcely knows that he has such a thing as a stomach. A man or woman with acute or chronic indigestion is conscious of his stomach every moment of the day and often most of the night. Every particle of nourishment taken in by the body for the supplying of animal warmth or muscular or mental strength, or the building of bone and flab, is taken througn tbe stomach. It the stomach is out of order the wnole body is unhealthy. To say nothing of the pain and inconvenience, the man with stomach trouble finds himself incapacitated for either work or pleasure. Why does a dyspeptic shiver with cold on the warmest day? Because his stomach cannot assimilate his food and supply the necessary heat. Why does a dyspeptic fail to fully enjoy life. Because his stomach cannot supply his body with strength. Cure the stomach and you cure the body.

magnified in success, if similarly inspired by the force and fervour of a living faith in God. But William Booth possessed also faith in himself. That may, perhaps, strike some as a doubt- ful quality, especially in a preacher of the Gospel. Yet preachels, whether they be men or women, can only be themselves, and are best when they are most entirely true to themselves. There is a self-conscious- ness which is vicious; but there also is a self- consciousness which is virtuous and which is essential to true power of influence and to success in all true service. A man, or a woman, can only realise and manifest the best there is in them when they throw all their energies and talents and powers into the work of their life. Timidity is the paralysis of endeavour. It is the °very courting of defeat. The successful teacher, or preacher, or worker of any kind, of necessity, must be courageous, bold, and brave. Countless were the hours, in this great Christian's history, when, but for his faith in his capacities and his opportunities, he must have gone down before the innumerable hostilities which he had to face. We who are Christian workers need similar Christian 'self- confidence if we are to reach the Christian success we desire. I do not hesitate to say that in all our Churches, Sunday schools, missions and brotherhoods, we all need a deeper confidence that God will make us increasingly useful in His service, if we will let Him ! We need to possess all the courage of our convictions as to truth, and as to duty, and as to opportunity ! As it is, we are too terribly apathetic, lethargic, idle, lazy ! We are not inventive enough in His service, we do not endure hardness, we do not believe our own beliefs. Probably we put out our very best energies in the things of this world, our business, cur pastimes, our social relationships, our politics, but we do not adequately believe in our spiritual powers, or recognise our opportunities of spiritual usefulness. All this General Booth, throughout his life, did believe in and believed that he could be enabled to achieve. "I can do all things through Christ who has strengthened I me." That was one of the manifest features of his whole career, and it qualified him to become the great leader, the great commander of a willing Army all over the world, which has won most splendid victories for Jesus Christ.

But General Booth, further, had faith in his fellow- men. He had faith in the supreme needs of their nature and their life. Ere studied, and got to know the inwardness as well as outwardness of men and women. He knew the natures and characters, with which his mission in the Gospel had sent him out to deal. It is exceedingly doubtful if any Christian Missionary of our time came in closer contact with spoiled lives, wrecked lives, degraded lives, lost lives ; than did this valiant leader in the Army of Jesus Christ. But, he acted upon the fixed belief that in the lowest and the worst of both men and women there was always an innate principle of right and truth, to wnich he could successfully appeal! He never imagined any human being too sunken in sin and degradation to be utterly unresponsive to the mercy and love of God, in Christ Jesus our Lord.. And that was how it ,came to pass that he, and his Army, have ever been enabled to enlist recruits, and make soldiers of righteousness of the vilest and the worst of sinners ! And also he had faith in the readiness and willingness of truly converted sinners to go in quest of the degraded, and sunken, and lost on every hand. General Booth made, by the help of God, even vilest outcasts, not only into re- spectable citizens, but into successful ingatherers of the still outcast and unsaved ! ! He believed in the humanity of all men and women; and knew that when once they had themselves been fully rescued and redeemed, and once felt the workings of divine grace in their own hearts, they would be willing, even eager, to go and seek to save their fellows! He even set himself against that hard selfishness, and self absorption, of too many professing Christians, which makes them content with the feeling of salvation for themselves, and careless of the bondage in sin and spiritual death of those around them. General Booth was the Leader and Commander of a Missionary Army every private of which was a soldier on active duty. never to be" retired," until the great King should bid him or her, "Go up higher." That was the essential principle of the Army, wonderfully embodied in the life of both its General and in the person of his ever memorable wife, and which still throbs and thrives in officers and rank and file of the Salva- tion Army. It needs to become the prominent characteristic of our Brotherhood, and of our churches of every name. Once again, General Booth bad unbounded faith in the Gospel, as the "power of God unto salvation.' How splendidly had he received all through the years evidence of the truth of his faith. We are told that there has never been any tabulated return of the actual number of soldiers all over the world, in the ranks of the Army. But there have been returns of the number of officers. Take these figures :—ln 1883 there were. 13 countries wherein the Army was at work, with 634 corps, and 1541 officers. In 1911 there were al countries, 8,972 corps, and 21,203 officers. So great, so widespread, so successful has been the work originated in the mind cf that one Christian believer, aided and accelerated by the unselfish devotion of his since sainted wife; and carried forward by the fervour, courage, self-sacrifice of the sinners saved under the flag of the Salvation Army. "What bath, God wrought ?" And now; the brave old General lies awaiting his burial ! "The General has laid down his Sword. God is with us ! " And there, by his coffin, his. " Sword " is laid. His Bible is laid there. "The Sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God." The one weapon by which he, and his soldiers, have won all their victories! Verily, his is a wonderful rec md. In some respects, unrivalled in history. How this. world, which cavilled, ridiculed, scorned at his person and his methods, now is busy and eloquent in its universal plaudit, " Well done! " And how that Wot ld above will have now received him to its eternal honours! "Servant of God, well done ! " Rest from thy loved employ; The battle fought, the victory won— Enter thy Master's joy.

THE LATE GENERAL BOOTH.

THE REV. JOHN YONGE'S TRIBUTE.

The following is a memorial address delivered by request to the Zion Chapel Brotherhood, Wakefield,by the Rev. John longs on Sunday last : V itbin the customary limits of this meeting, it is almost, if not quite, impossible to compress a worthy estimation of the truly great Christain soldier who has just passed away. What has sufficed to fill columns, and even pages, of the Daily Press can hardly find adequate expression here to-day. I under- stand, however, that your Brotherhood desires to baar testimony to the wonderful character, and worth, and work of General Booth • and hence, as I happen to be visiting this pulpit to-Clay, I have been asked to give expression to at least some of the thoughts and feel- ings which are so universally prevalent. Now, it would, no doubt, have been better if either your President (Mr. J. A. Yonge) or the minister of this church (Rev. G. Slack) had been at home and able to fulfil this duty. In the absence of both, however, I will do the best I can t frame an apprsciatii n of a long and eventful career of a great and even world- wide ministry, within the inadequate space alone possible now. No doubt many, or even all, of you have seen the references in the public Press, to the life and labours of General Booth. I suppose the public prints which , have been entirely silent have been exceedingly few and unimportant. Nearly all pape•s have borne witness to his worth and his unselfishness.

The "Manchester Guardian" has finely said that "the religious world has lost in the death of General Booth, its most heroic and picturesque figure. Evangelist, Reformer, Statesman, as rich in creative resource as he was resolute in execution, and combining with the cold mental calculation of an engineer, a fiery religious enthusiasm which set every- thing it touched ablaze, he takes a high place among the founders of religicus orders in the Western world. . . . His career was a Religious Romance. In the sixties of the last century, he was an obscure evangelist, in the east end of Loudon ; to-day his name is known, and his followers are at work, in all parts of the world. It is not his opinions, his methods, or his singularities, which the public recall to-day. They think of him as a man of rare faith, who felt he had a Mission to the poor and the godless of the earth, and who dedicated body and soul to its prosecution." Now, he appears to have been exemplary from his youth. One could only wish that young fellows of our day could enter upon life with a tenth or a httndreth part of the zeal and ambition with which he did. Appealing once to a companicin, the youth said "Have you no ambition ?" His companion wonderingly asked his meaning. "Because I have. I intend to be something great. Ido not mean to belong to the commonalty ! " It sounded bold and even boastful ; but it is the lads who honestly set forth with something of that spirit who make their mark in the world. The General has told how he was ever in favour of good works, and of helping people who were in trouble. "I had unutterable yearnings," he hays, "to do something to minister to their needs." Note how the boy was father to the man. Just when he was in that condition of mind, he was converted to God. In the slums and pavilions of Nottingham, I learned to speak and talk in my own way. He seems to have once cherished some idea of the Congrega- tional ministry, but he soon saw that his sphere did not lie there, and he therefore entered into the ministry of the Methodist New Connexion. Just at that time be became engaged to be married, and the choice which he made deliberately, and in accordance with a carefully formed ideal, had infinitely to do, not only with his personal happenings, but with the marvellous success of his entire career. They say, and very truly, that "a man is what a woman makes him," and had William Booth chosen a mate of other and lemer character, his life had probably been a life of vastly other and smaller history. At the annual Conference of his church in 1861. the majority were against his wish ta withdraw from regular pastoral work, and they suggested some plan whereby he might be continued in it. But his wife rose unexpectedly in the gallery, and shouted " Never !" Booth instantly bade the conference fare- well ; his wife met him at the door, and those two united and consecrated spirits went out to find andl fashion their own unhampered and independent career. They went out from home, and from salary, with four children under five years of age, and faced a life which had only such prospects as intrepid courage and apostolic zeal could procure. He began very largely in the open-air, until he took an old theatre on the stage of which there soon regularly mounted 40, 50, 60 sinners on a Sunday night, seeking mercy ! From that time, his work" caught on," with rapid and spreading success. Now, it is abundantly manifest, that William Booth began, continued, and ended his career, as a man of fixed, undaunted, and tuadatintab!e faith! Remember, how he cast himself adrift from all recognized Christian communities! He was bound by no bonds to any church or any denomination. That initial bet raised all kinds of doubts about him, and created innumerable difficulties for him. But he proved the truth of the apostolic assertion, even that " This is the victory which overcometh the world, even our faith."

He manifested everywhere a living faith in God. His was not a mere mental belief about God, but a personal faith in the personal God, revealed in Jesus Christ. His faith was spiritual sight. It was that ' which inspired him with such heroic cmfidence, as enabled him, along with his wonderful wife, and along with the converts who gathered around him, to pro- secute his great career. There is, I fear, hardly a Christian worker, of any grade, in the ranks of our churches, who has possessed and proved such a cour- age of conviction, such an assurance of divine favour and upholding, as did that plain man and his wife, from the very outset of their career ! Theirs was a faith in God "which laughs at impossibilities, and says it shall be done !" Faith in the reality of God. Faith in the actual presence of God. Faith in the power of the Divine Spirit in the humblest and even , unworthiest believing heart, giving effect to tha , ' Message of Mercy, and with the teaching of the truth, • as the truth is, in Jesus. With Booth and his wife. 1 the work they did, the word they declared, the influence they continually exerted, were ever of God! Hence it was that their messages were with power, and crowds of the ungodly fell under the sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God. It is simply impossible otherwise to explain the influence of their labours, the power of their preaching. They were inspired of the Hely Ghost, and spake words of love, truth, tenderness, boldness and fire, as the Spirit gave them utterance. Beyond all doubt, therein lay the service of their power and the explanation of their success. I venture to say that there is not a Church o' any name, or a ministry of any accomplishment, or talent, or power of eloquence, but would be vastly

BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Rev. A. M. Mitchell and Stimulants.

'The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning, Mr E L Bent presiding. There were also present Messrs A .1 Jolley, J Billinge, A Brookes, W C Brooks, Alderman James Evans, W Flanagan, W Furness, W Houghton. J A Hughes, J W Knowles, G Mather, Rev A M Mitchell Thomas Ritchie, J A Rusliworth, Mrs Simpson, A 13ottomley (clerk), R L Partridge (assistant clerk) and W .1 Brown (Master). The Padgate Cottage Homes and Schools Committee whose minutes were approved placed on record their regret at the severance of a long connection about to take place by the retlrement on pension of Mr Wright the superintendent of the homes and they extended to him every good wish for his future life trusting that he would have good health and every happiness. After the minutes had been passed the Rev A M MITCHELL referred to a paragraph relating to the corporal punishment of a boy, and said as to the corporal punishment referred to in those minutes— The CHAIRMAN : These minutes are now passed Mr Mr MITCHELL : It is arising out of the minutes. The CHAIRMAN : They are passed. Rev A M MiTcus:tr. : Quite so but (cries of order and next business) but you asked a few moments ago was there any further discussion. The CHAIRMAN : That was before the minutes had been passed. (Several voices: Next business). The Rev Gentleman was about to resume his speech but he was shouted down and Mrs Simpson remarked to him that they would never allow him to speak. It was autocratic government. The next business VMS then proceeded with. At a meeting of the Finance Committee on the 23rd August, the Clerk's estimate of eypenditnre for the half-year ending 30th March, 1913, was presented and considered. It was reported that owing to the increased expenditare during the past year caused by the industrial unrest, the balance in the hands of the treasurer had been materially reduced, and having that fact in mind they considered it would be inadvisable to defray the cost of the heating scheme out of current account, as to do so would in all probability mean an increase of the rates. They recommended the Board to approve the Clerk's estimate save and except tbe item of £l5OO for building and repairs ; to ask the Local Government Board to authorise the borrowing of a loan of £2,902 to defray the cost of the scheme for heating at the Workhouse ; such loan to be repaid within five years.—They also recommended the Board to approve the recommendation of the Padgate Homes and Schools Committee to add 10 years to the service which Mr and Mrs Fairhurst had served for the pur- pose of computing their superannuation allowance. Mr. JOLLEY said it would be within the recollection of the Board that after years of consideration and dis- cussion it was decided to ask the Local Government Board to sanction an altered system of heating in the workhouse or substitute for the existing methods, which were somewhat unsatisfactory, revised methods which would effect very substantial economies. The Local Government Board sent down one of their engineering inspectors to inquire as to the scheme. He had come down prejudiced against the suggested altera- tions. After having gone carefully into them with some members of the committee who devoted a great deal of time to the work he was fully satisfied in spite of the initial prejudice that the scheme was a very admirable one and would effect substantial economies in fuel consumption and management. As a result of that the Local Government Board gave their sanction to the scheme and the Guardians had entered into a contract for the carrying out of the work. The engineer bad come to the conclusion that some points could be revised and on his advice and the sanction of the Local Government Board, those alterations were made. Those alterations brought the cost of the scheme up to £2,902. If things had gone on under normal con- ditions it would have been practicable to have paid that capital expenditure out of the accumulated balance of,revenue, but 12 months ago the 3ountry was upset through industrial disturbances which bad affected the outrelief expenditure. Six months ago there were other disturbances which further materially added to the expenditure. The result of that had been that the balance in hand was £2,000 less than it would have been under normal conditions, The work was being pushed forward and would be completed before Christmas. If it was to be paid for out of revenue there was no way out of it than to make an additional call equal to a rate of 2d. in the £ on the rates of the various townships. The alternatve was to borrow and repay in five years.

A BIRD'S-EYE VIEW OF THE TRAFFIC IN MAGDALEN-STREET.

The real issue was, did the Guardians think it desirable to add to the call of the overseers of the various towns or did they think it better to ask for the sanction of the Local Government Board to borrow the money and repay it in five years. He proposed that the minutes and recommendations be approved. He did not think there would be any discussion about the superannuation of the retiring porter and portress. They all knew of the valuable services they had rendered. (Hear; hear). Mr. MATHER seconded and the minutes and recom- mendations were adopted.

should be left entirely to the Medical Officer. If the Board does not think it is satisfactorily administered then there is a need for another resolution from Mr Mitchell or someone else to further inquire into the whole matter.

Mr Rusu WORTH said they could not interfere with the medical officer in the discharge of his duties. Was it not a waste of time to talk about nonsense?

The CLERK said that if Mr Mitchell had explained to him exactly what he require he would have pointed out to him the exact position of affairs. It was that the alcoholic liquors were charged through the Masters' provision book. In that book was shown the stock received and also a record of the amounts given out. When the doctor gave an order for any .stimulent the amount was entered upon the bed card of each patient and from those cards the master reckoned up the quantity used. If it was the wish of Mr Mitchell or anyone to know the quantities consumed it would 1-e necessary to produce the doctor's orders and the cards. There was actually no particular book k.pt. Mr KNowLes : Then it means more labour and expense, and we are putting enough on the ratepayers at the present time. The CHAIRMAN : I think we are all agreed that the book should be laid on the table. (Cries of " No, no.") Mr JOLLEY : We are not all agreed in any sense. In the first instance there is no such book.

Rev A M MITCHELL-: Then there ought to be. Mr JOLLEY : If Mr Mitchell is honest and sincere in what he is aiming at, he ought not to shout, but move that such a book be kept. He puts his text down and his sermon has no connection with it. Rev A M MITCHELL : If you will allow me I will withdraw it and substitute another. Several members of the Pe lard expressed the desire that the motion should be put to the meeting, and this was agreed to.—On a vote being taken, only the Rev A M M,tchell and Mrs Simps-m voted for the resolution, and 10 other members voted against.
