THE NEW PORTER AND _FORTRESS.

Candidates for the positions of porter and portress of the Cottage Homes were then interviewed and Mr and Mrs James Worrell, of Padgate were appointed. Having given notice at the previous meeting the Rev A M MITChELL moved " that the book of the Medical Officer of the Workhouse be laid on the table at every fortnightly meeting of the Board" and in doing so said he did not think it was any credit to the Board that there should be any necessity for the moving of such a resolution. There might be times and occasions when alcohol might be served with a distinct purpose but it ought to be remembered that there was a danger which existed there, of extravagancies in the use of alcohol. He belonged to the Order of Rechabits and that Order allowed the use of stimulants under a medical certificate. It was rather the medical men they had to deal with and consider in connection with that motion. Many people had been doubly thankful for the statements of leading medical authorities in connection with the liritisix Medical Association— The CLERK : L thought you were moving a resolution Rev A M MITCHELL : SO I am. The Chairman : But you are going astray from the point. Rev A M MlTcrtimr, : lam not going astray at all. Mr. KNOWLES : This is rather in the nature of a teetotal sermon. Rev A M MITCIIELL: I wanted to show you that the use of alcohol which obtains in our own particular infirmary is wrong. The CHAIRMAN : I must object if you are going to do that. If you are going to move a resolution you must move it. I shall not allow you to proceed when you are continually criticising the action of the medical officer. You have no right to do it. Rev MITCHELL : I move the resolution. I don't ' know whether tha word " book " is absolutely correct. The book I want putting on the table is that in which there is a record of the use of alcohol and to see how many patients it is administered and in what quantities The book ought to correspond with the requisition hol which is before the House Committee on Fridayk Latterly be had noticed—he would not say there was anything particular in it—that the quantities had nots been specified for liquors in the requisition book for the last-- Mr lit;owLl:s : Surely this is a matter for the Local Government Board. We are not supposed to sit here and listen to this trash of yours, Mr Mitchell. The CHAIRMAN : I cinnot allow you to proceed in that sort of way, Mr Mitchell. Rev Altrnit Ent. : I move the resolution. The CHAIRMAN : Yes, but you are not moving it. You are going astray from the question in every par- ticular. Mrs SIMPSON seconded the rev gentleman's resolution The CHAIRMAN said he was not opposed to the book being laid on the table if it was necessary. The book, however, was kept within ten yards from that room and Mr Mitchell would not take the trouble to examine it.

Rev MiTCHELt.: Certainly not. The CHAIRMAN : At the Sallle time you are criticis- ing the action of the doctor by saying he is administer- ing a thing he has no right to do. If that is correct and if you write to the Local Government Board you have your own remedy. You will soon get your answer to mind your own business. That is what you ought to do. Alderman EVAN did not see why the particular book should not be laid on the table like any other book. Mr. W. C. BROOKS: But he does not know which book he wants in. . .

Alderman EVANS took it that the book that was wanted by the rev, gentleman was that in which there was a record of the stimulants given. Mr. KNOWLES : And all prescriptions. Alderman EVANS had no objection so far as the book was concerned but when it came to the further point as to who was to be the prescriber of the quantity to be given.--Was it to be the Medical Officer ? Mr. KNOW LS : Or the Vicar ? Alderman EVANS : Or Mr Mitchell or any other member of the Board then it is a different matter. If Mr Mitchell wants this book for the purpose of know- ing bow much of any particular liquor is consumed in the ho:pital he has a perfect right to have the hook on the table. THE TERRITORIALS. Extracts From Western Command Orders. Appreciation of the Work done by Territorial _ _

Troops In Camp, 1912.

The Army Council have conveyed to the General- Officer-Couaruatiding-iu-Chief their appreciation and that of the Secretary of nate for War for the excel- lent spirit which was shown by the Territorial Troops in camp this year. The weather has been most inclement, and the soldierly spirit in whlch the troops bore their di,comforts was most praiseworthy.

Wycliffe P.S A. Society. There was a good meeting of this society on Sunday last presided over by the President Mr. B. Farrar. Rev. F. M. Bobenizer gave the address upon "Courage," in a most interesting manner. There was a courage he said which was simply bravado. Christian life needed courage. We should have seasons that Peter had, when courage failed him, we needed to stand firm, stand straight facing the foe for righteousness sake: cling to the right decided by conscience looking to the great Helper in every time of need. Miss E. Tanner sang a hymn, "The best friend to have is Jesus," and 'Nearer my God to Thee" in a most tasteful manner ; Mr Payton presided at the organ. Mr. Jos. Owen read the lesson, and the chairman led the prayer. Two new members received a hearty welcome.

Emigration to Canada this year has been well maio- tained. An oflicial cable advice from Ottawa states that during the first thrE,e mouths, April to June, of the pre- sent fiscal year, the immigration figures have been as follows :—British, 69,919 ; United States, 53,343 ; Continental. 52,034 ; total, 175,346. This result com- pares with 120,353 tor the corresponding period in 1911. It is added that the arrivals during July and August are known to have been on an equally. favourable scale, and it is believer' that the record emigration into Canada last year will he exceeded. From the above figures the immigration of aliens into Canada considerably exceeded those of British stock. Technically this is true, but when one considers the com- position of the emigration received from the United States it transpires that the greater proportion consists of returned Canadians and others of English parentage, added to which a considerable percentage of native-born Americans, all of whom represent Anglo- Saxon civilisation, and who will, naturally, combine with the British element already in Canada in assimilating the immigration coming from the more ba:dcward races of Europe.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Smith—Watkins.

An interesting but quiet wedding was solemnised at Holy Trinity Church, on Tuesday 'morning. Despite the fact that both families desired the ceremony to be as quiet as possible the church was well filled with the many friends of the contracting parties, who were Mr. George H. Smith, of Lovely-lane, hon. secretary to the Conservative Association, and Miss Florrie Watkins, daughter of Mrs. Watkins, Sankey-street. The bride- groom is well-known in local operatic circles and is also prominently identified with the Warrington Cycling Club from whom he was the recipient of a handsome present on Friday week last. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. L. Parry. The bride was given away by her mother. She was attended by Miss Gladys Watkins as bridesmaid, and the best man was Mr. F. Barber. A reception was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe. The honeymoon will be spent in the Lake District.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Cox—Jones.

Much interest was manifested in a very pretty local wedding which took place at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday. The contracting parties were Miss Ada Jones, second daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Edward Jones, of 36, Sankey-street, Warrington, and Mr. George Harry Cox, third son of Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Cox, of Wigan. The cfficiating clergyman was the Rev. Hugh VVilliamson, and the duties of best man were discharged by Mr. Arthur Lake, cousin of the bride. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a gown of ivory chiffon taffeta, handsomely embroidered, also a beautiful veil, the work and gift of her mother. She carried a bouquet of white flowers and was attended by four bridesmaids, the Misses Irene and Ruth Barbour (nieces of the bride), Miss Ethel Maddock (niece of the bridegroom), and Miss Minnie Jones (sister of the bride.) The first three wore cream silk hand embroidered dresses, with caps to match, and their ornaments were gold bangles the gift of the bridegroom. Miss Jones wore a dress of pink shot chiffon Taffeta with Juliet cap, her orna- ment being a watch bracelet the gift of the bridegroom. The church was crowded with friends and well-wishers and appropriate wedding music was supplied by Mr Hickman. After the ceremony the wedding breakfast was served at Atkinson's Cafe, Bridge-street, where about 40 guests were entertained. Later in the day Mr and Mrs Cox, who were the recipients of numerous and costly presents, left for the Lake District where the honeymoon is being spent. On their return they will take up their residence at Platt Fields,Manchester.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
SILVER SEAL IN THE DEE. CAPTURED AND SHOT.

A splendid specimen of a silver grey seal, resembling a Labrador dog seal, three feet long, has been found in the Dee estuary by Mr. Milner, a fisherman, of Park- gate. It was alive when caught, but died shortly after- wards, having apparently been shot by some person on the bank. In all probability it has travelled with the icebergs into the Atlantic and been carried into the Dee estuary.

THE STEAM ROLLER FATALITY AT WIDNES. Mr. Coroner Brighonse held an inquest at Widnes on Saturday on George Alfred Davies, aged fourteen, residing at 17, Farnworth-street, who met his death on Tuesday last by being run over by a scarifier attached to a steam roller chartered by the Lancashire County Council for road work. It appeared that deceased, along with three other boys, upon their return from work at the Sutton Manor Colliery, near Ratnhill, mounted the scarifier, which was pulled in tow by the road engine. The deceased was the last to get on, and after being warned by the conductor to keep away from the machine was caught in the wheel and dragged under. He was terribly i crushed about the body, and died n his brother's arms. The brother had passed the engine fifty yards when he heard the screams of the victim. . The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death." and added a rider to the effect that the conductor of the scarifier should possess a horn of sufacient power i to alarm the driver of the engine n front.

===== 0002601_19120831_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Paltry Progress.

Sir,—The letter of Mr. Charles Dukes is a fair statement of the orthodox socialist position. The views he holds are held by quite a large number of people in this and other countries. Mr. Dukes says : "Labour is in the position of an army constantly at war yet burdened with a majority of its soldiers who refuse to take up arms in their own defence." Socialists often refer to this apathy of the masses, yet they keep on preaching to these same inert people in the hope, apparently, that a miracle will happen. Some day they expect that the masses will be roused to action, when a revolution will occur out of which will arise a new social order.

Society has always consisted of a large inert mass of people and a few active and go-ahead individuals This difference is temperamental and not the result of environment. Circumstances may help to develope the agitator, the inventor or the originator but they do not create him.

In the same way the great mass of workers are boni inert. They lack the energy and the imagina- tion to appreciate the ideas of the few. They resist all change and only when a thing has become estab- lished do they appreciate it. These two types of temperament each find it hard to understand the other. All through history the masses have crucified ,burned or strayed the benefactors of the race. And the agitators or leaders have always cursed the masses for their apathy. Is it not time that socialists recognised these facts and gave up the idea of a wide-spread revolt among the workers ? Social evolution prcceeds by a series of steps. A few bold spirit S develop an idea in common which they proceed to materialise. After many attempts they establish something which hears a resemblance to their original ideas. Then having been proved and tested by experience it is taken up by the masses. The great bulk of the people are always afraid of the unknown and the untried. Progress is alwajs made by some few initiating and the mass eventually endorsing. Now the workers of to-day are living under a system of capitalism. The great majority of them are incapable of conceiving any thing better. The idea of collectivism is to them the wildest of dreams. Men are always willing to work and sacrifice for that which they think is attainab'e and the fact that men will not work for socialism proves that to their way of think- ing it is not a practical proposition. But socialists believe in socialism. To them it is something to which man may reasonably aspire. They never saw or felt it yet they hold that it is not only possible but inevitable.

Now if it be true that progress is made by example and endorsement u hat should be the socialists' line of action ? When they preach to the workers they make no impression because the average worker is impressed only by what he can touch and see. Cannot socialists give the workers something to touch and handle? Socialists are not few in numbers Many capitalist firms are run successfully with a few hundred men. What is to prevent socialists from organising them- selves in thousands?

Democratically controlled industry will only displace the present despotic type when it can be made more econoadcally efficient. Capitalism will go as soon as the woi kers are capable of industrial self-organisation and not before. Some workers will be able to organise before the general mass and should not these be the socialists ?

If socialists would cease prodding the apathetic worker, grumbling at the "system " er heaping scorn on the wicked capitalist, and would give a lead towards industrial self-organisation they would earn the lasting gratitude of mankind. Stockham, Runcorn. , r

W. BAGULEY.

Sir,—l hope Mr. Dukes will forgive me if I say that I was more interested in his letter than convinced of the utility of strikes as a means of social re-adjust- ment. I have carefully read his letter to find out how the strike does remeay economic inequality, as Mr. Dukes so dogmatically asserts, and I cannot, be as open minded as I will, find out where Mr. Dukes comes to grips with the questions. We will not trouble about the present time when the workers are not organised sufficiently to monopolise their labour power but will pass on to the time when they will be, to see what this will bring us to. Our trade union friends declare a strike and get their rise in wages. These arrangements seem alright, but what about the economic factors? To quote Mr Dukes, he says: "The worker who enters into the employ of a firm agrees to sell his labour for so much money which means so much bread, beer and clothes or common necessaries of life. When increased prices and competition amongst the workers for jobs have reduced these conditions to a low level (and this is constantly going on), they come together and agree to demand increased pay, which means better conditions." You see here that even Mr. Dukes admits that the economic factor operates against the worker. In the first place he admits that there is a constant rise in prices of commodities and in the second place a great surplus of labour power which must reduce wages to the lowest level of subsistence. This is an economic theorem which the Socialists have expounded for years. At one time they asserted that so long as there were men at the gate waiting for the other man's job, wages would always remain at the lowest possible level, but now, a great proportion of the Socialists have forgotten their original theories and plump for the strike.

Will Mr. Dukes show us bow (if increasing prices and a surplus of labour power neutralise the increase in wages gained by strikes) how strikes are going to readjust economic inequality? My further objection to strikes is that their objec- tive—better wages—is economically unsound. The standard of life does not depend upon the amount of wages we get but upon the amount and quality of commodities exchanged for our wages. Will a rise in wages guarantee a rise in the state of life for the workman? I believe that if the worker obtained 100 per cent, advance in wages to-morrow by the strike or any other means, all the subterranean facilities of capitalist society would be put into operation immediately to multiply the effect that an increase of wages would have under prevailing circumstances. So long as the capitalist class own the means of making the eternal dividend the demand for more wages will have little effect. It may render capitalist society a little unstable. It may break into that perfect tranquillity that the capitalist has enjoyed for so long. Take our trade union friends of to-day as an example. They are organised. They get on the average more wages than the unorganised. But do they get more wages because they are organised or because more wages will keep them contented so that they will not be in constant conflict with capitalism, and in the process understand more of capitalism than is good for the capitalist class ? A thoroughly organised working class would put a different aspect upon the question of labour organised only for the strike. If the worker accepts present- day society, with an occasional strike for better wages, as his only conception of existence, the capitalist possesses the might (therefore right) if might means right Pr vice versa, to defeat the worker every time. If the capitalist's dividend depends upon starving the workers into submission, you can depend upon it he is going to do so in spite of his professed Christianity. Bistory teaches us that this class has always been able to adapt itself according to circumstance. The dominant class understand their economic and political power. The workers do not. They have always been able to defeat the the workers, because the workers have always adopted revolt and not intelligent adoption to circumstances. If the worker does not understand that the first essential to a humane existence is that the land from which all our material necessities are derived should be owned by the people, and that the means of extracting wealth from the land should also be owned by people, well then, it•is the people's loss. If the workers want to better their condition they must aim at getting the means of procuring better conditions under their own control and not deal with superficiali- ties. There is no need to take up arms and fight. The I enemy of the workers is their own ignorance. The I capitalist power lies in the ignorance of the people.— Yours, &c.,

FEWER PAUPERS IN LANCASHIRE. A return issued by Mr. Arthur Lowry, the Local Government Board inspector for Lancashire, Cumber- land, and Westmorland. shows that in Lancashire on the first day of this year 41,935 persons were in receipt of outdoor relief. This is a great decrease, for on the first day of lest year the number was 51,634, and on the first day of 1910 it was 54,130.
